# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.2 PAGE 1

## PROJECT PROGRAMME SUMMARY

### A. Project objectives

#### A.1. Overall project objective
Reduce GHG emissions by 50% through improved energy efficiency and renewable energy adoption.

#### A.2. Specific project outcomes (OPFs)
- Improved energy efficiency in buildings.
- Increased use of renewable energy sources.
- Enhanced climate resilience.

### B. Mitigation co-benefits

#### B.1. Energy generation and access
- 50% reduction in GHG emissions.
- 40% increase in renewable energy usage.

#### B.2. Buildings, cities, industries, and appliances
- Improved efficiency standards.
- Increased adoption of green technologies.

#### B.3. Forestry and land use
- Preservation of natural habitats.
- Reduced deforestation rates.

#### B.4. Health and well-being, and food and water security
- Enhanced public health outcomes.
- Improved access to clean water.

#### B.5. Disaster risk reduction and resilience
- Early warning systems implemented.
- Resilience building measures adopted.

### C. Results framework

#### C.1. Expected mitigation outcomes
- 30 million metric tons CO2e reduced annually by 2030.
- 50% increase in renewable energy capacity by 2030.

#### C.2. Expected co-benefits
- Improved public health outcomes.
- Enhanced climate resilience measures.

### D. Time frames

#### D.1. Time frame for GEF funding
- 90 months USD.

#### D.2. Time frame for GEF loaned funds
- 28 months USD.

### E. Project size
- Large (over USD 20 million).

## India's Energy Transition Challenge

1. **India's CO2 Emissions**
   - India is the world’s third-largest CO2 emitter with over 8% of its primary energy supply in 2023 being derived from coal.
   - The country has committed to reducing emissions by 45% below 2005 levels by 2030.

2. **India's Clean Energy Transition**
   - India aims to achieve 175 GW of renewable energy capacity by 2022, with a target of 450 GW by 2030.
   - The country has committed to reducing the carbon intensity of its economy by 33-35% by 2030.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.1 | PAGE 3

We'd like to provide a brief overview of the proposal before delving into details about technology and financial development—such as Round-the-Clock (RTC) technologies, green hydrogen, compressed biogas, and carbon capture utilization and storage (CCUS). These are new challenges. New approaches are required for industrial decarbonization, net-zero transition, and related storage technologies. We aim to provide a comprehensive overview of the latest developments in these areas.

## 1. Introduction

The high total cost of technology, such as off-takers in one experiment, high risk premiums, and downstream market challenges, is a major barrier to widespread adoption. Various financial instruments are available to address this issue. The Asian Development Bank (ADB) has developed a range of innovative financing solutions that can be used to support the development of new technologies. These include:

- **Green Climate Fund (GCF)**: A fund established by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) to support projects and programs in developing countries that help mitigate or adapt to climate change.
- **Climate Investment Funds (CIF)**: A network of funds designed to accelerate a shift toward low-carbon, climate-resilient development.

## 2. Proposed Intervention

GCF proposed to the Asian Development Bank (ADB) is pursuing India's leading development finance institution with a focus on green technology and renewable energy. The ADB's Green Climate Fund (GCF) aims to leverage $1 billion from ADB and leading industrial development banks, raising $3.5 billion in total.

### 2.1 GCF Proposal

The ADB's GCF proposal includes the following key elements:

- **RTC Technologies**: Round-the-Clock (RTC) technologies are designed to provide continuous power.
- **Green Hydrogen**: Green hydrogen is produced using renewable energy sources, reducing carbon emissions.
- **CCUS**: Carbon Capture Utilization and Storage (CCUS) technologies capture CO2 emissions from industrial processes and store them underground.

### 2.2 Financial Instruments

The ADB's GCF proposal includes a range of financial instruments designed to support the development of new technologies. These include:

- **Loans**: Low-interest loans provided by the ADB.
- **Grants**: Grants provided by the GCF for specific projects.
- **Equity Investments**: Equity investments in renewable energy companies.

### 2.3 Risk Mitigation

The ADB's GCF proposal includes a range of risk mitigation measures designed to support the development of new technologies. These include:

- **Political Risk Insurance**: Provided by the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA).
- **Credit Enhancement**: Provided by the ADB.
- **Technical Assistance**: Provided by the GCF.

## 3. Conclusion

The ADB's GCF proposal is designed to support the development of new technologies in India. The proposal includes a range of financial instruments and risk mitigation measures designed to support the development of new technologies. The proposal also includes a range of innovative financing solutions designed to support the development of new technologies.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (V3.2) PAGE 4

## The proposal for IFF

- **Targeting underserved technologies and geographies:** IFF is uniquely positioned to direct capital towards underserved markets, including emerging economies in Africa, LATAM, and Asia-Pacific. IFF will promote the adoption of innovative technologies such as RTC (real-time computing), IoT (Internet of Things), AI (artificial intelligence), and blockchain.

- **Empowering DPFs as climate finance leaders:** Annualized global revenues of development banks are ~$10 trillion, with ~25% allocated to climate-related activities. IFF will empower DPFs to become leaders in climate finance by providing them with the tools and resources needed to achieve their goals.

- **Deploying online, scalable financial instruments:** The IFF will provide customized debt financing with a focus on climate resilience and sustainability. This includes green bonds, sustainability-linked loans, and other innovative financial solutions.

- **Meeting capacity needs and policy gaps:** By leveraging expertise and demonstrating best practices, IFF will help DPFs build capacity to meet the challenges of climate change. This includes providing technical assistance and training to DPFs.

- **Institutionalizing green finance principles:** IFF intends to address the need for a global standard that harmonizes the various approaches to green finance. This includes working with multilateral development banks (MDBs), regional development banks, and other financial institutions to develop a common framework.

### Climate Results/Benefits

- The IFF is designed to deliver transformative climate impacts by addressing mitigation needs and supporting adaptation in a cost-effective manner. A key outcome of the facility will be the acceleration of greenhouse gas emissions reductions, with support for projects that avoid, reduce, or remove GHG emissions. This includes financing for renewable energy, energy efficiency, sustainable transport, and other low-carbon technologies. IFF will also support the development of innovative climate solutions, including carbon capture and storage (CCS), nature-based solutions, and enhanced long-term sustainability.

### Paradigm Shift

- **Catalyzing Financial System Transformation:** IFF is designed to address deep-rooted structural barriers that hinder the flow of capital towards sustainable investments. By leveraging the expertise of DPFs, IFF will catalyze a transformation in the financial system by addressing practices and norms. From conventional finance towards a sustainable finance paradigm, the program aims to enhance capacity for DPFs to adopt and implement green finance principles. This includes providing technical assistance, funding, and resources needed to achieve this transition.

- **Enhancing Technical Assistance:** IFF will provide DPFs with the tools and frameworks needed to accelerate, structure, and manage green investments. This includes providing technical assistance, funding, and resources for DPFs to adopt and implement green finance principles. IFF (operating with funds Business Responsibility and Sustainability Reporting (BRSR) framework), IFF will enable DPFs to enhance their capacity to deliver on the objectives of the Paris Agreement.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.2 PAGE 1

## A

### 11. "Driving Benefits: Inclusive Market Transitions" (GBF) serves as a demonstrator platform for replicable and scalable solutions to drive inclusive market transitions.

- **Objective:** GBF aims to reduce the carbon footprint of the most vulnerable communities by providing access to clean energy, reducing waste in supply chains, and facilitating fair trade.
- **Approach:** GBF supports women-led enterprises, youth groups, indigenous peoples, and local governments. It promotes inclusive economic growth through targeted investments, bridging gender gaps, empowering marginalized groups, and enabling systemic change across sectors.
- **Outcome:** GBF aims to achieve a predicted mitigation impact of 10 MtCO2e with a strong path to high-impact finance deployment. GBF positions itself as a foundational element in the new climate economy.

### Key Features:

- **Inclusive Approach:** GBF is designed to not exclude any group, ensuring that marginalized and high-risk clean energy enterprises are supported.
- **Gender Equality:** GBF is committed to gender equality and women's empowerment. It aims to support at least 50% of all the enterprises with a focus on women-led businesses.
- **Capacity Building:** GBF supports capacity building for local governments, foundations, and other stakeholders to ensure long-term sustainability and impact.

### c) Indicator: energy sector emissions

Energy sector emissions are driven by a heavy dependence on fossil fuels. India's energy supply relies on three major sources: coal, oil, and natural gas. A dominant role is played by the industrial and transport sectors, contributing to greenhouse gas emissions. Agriculture also plays a significant role in greenhouse gas emissions.

The energy sector has been identified as one of the most important contributors to climate change. The energy sector accounts for 70% of total CO2 emissions in India. ## B

**Author:** [Your Name], and [Co-author's Name]

**Date:** [Date]

**Page:** 7

India has been a pioneer in the field of renewable energy. Extreme weather conditions like droughts can severely impact the agricultural sector, which is the backbone of India’s economy. The country has also witnessed an increase in energy requirements due to rapid industrialization and urbanization. To mitigate these challenges, India has adopted various measures such as solar energy, wind power, hydroelectricity, biomass, and geothermal energy.

### Summary of physical climate risks in India

India faces significant climate risks driven by rising temperatures, altered precipitation patterns, and increased frequency of extreme weather events:

- **Temperature rise:** The average temperature in India is projected to increase by 1.5°C to 2.0°C by the end of this century.
- **Heatwaves:** Heatwaves are expected to become more frequent and intense, with a significant impact on human health and agriculture.
- **Precipitation changes:** India’s monsoon system is highly sensitive to climate change, leading to increased variability in rainfall patterns.

### Recognizing the importance of energy transition

India has made ambitious commitments to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and increase renewable energy adoption. The country aims to achieve 40% cumulative electric power installed capacity from non-fossil fuel-based energy resources by 2030 and ensure that at least 50% of its total energy requirements come from renewable sources.

- **Solar energy:** India has set a target of 175 GW of renewable energy capacity by 2022, including 60 GW from wind power and 100 GW from solar power.
- **Energy efficiency:** The country is also focusing on improving energy efficiency in various sectors to reduce overall energy consumption.

### Key initiatives

India has implemented several policies and programs to support its renewable energy goals:

- **National Solar Mission:** Launched in 2010, this mission aims to establish India as a global leader in solar energy by creating the policy conditions for solar technology diffusion across the country.
- **Wind Energy Targets:** The government has set ambitious targets for wind energy capacity addition, aiming to reach 60 GW by 2022.

### Challenges and opportunities

Despite significant progress, several challenges remain:

- **Financing:** Securing adequate financing remains a critical challenge for scaling up renewable energy projects.
- **Infrastructure:** Improving grid infrastructure is essential to integrate renewable energy sources effectively.
- **Public awareness:** Increasing public awareness about the benefits of renewable energy can drive adoption.

### Conclusion

India’s commitment to renewable energy and climate resilience demonstrates its leadership in addressing global climate challenges. By continuing to invest in renewable energy and improving energy efficiency, India can achieve its sustainability goals while mitigating the impacts of climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND TRANSFORMS PAGE 1

## Key functions and technologies must be targeted to accelerate the energy transition. Supporting policy (often missing) is essential for success.

- **Technology deployment:** Key technologies are needed to reduce emissions, improve job growth, enhance security, and an improved quality of life. The benefits are key outcomes.
    - **Electricity production:** Accelerating for approximately 65% of global emissions, represents the highest priority for climate action.
        - **Renewable energy:** Converting 95% of low-emissions electricity generation to renewable sources by 2030.
            - **Hydrogen:** Hydrogen-based technologies can be deployed in the range of 1,000-1,500 TWh/year by 2050.
        - **Energy efficiency:** Energy efficiency is a key component of climate action and must be targeted for deployment.
    - **Agri-technology sector:** 65% of all energy consumption from agriculture can prevent adverse applications in agriculture and forestry. This supports the development of the food and fiber sectors, prevents adverse outcomes for farmers, and reduces emissions.

## 1.2. Policy frameworks

- **Policy frameworks** are needed to accelerate climate action.
    - **Carbon pricing:** Carbon pricing mechanisms are needed to accelerate climate action.
        - **Carbon tax:** A carbon tax is a key mechanism to accelerate climate action.
            - **Carbon tax:** A carbon tax is a key mechanism to accelerate climate action.
                - **Carbon tax:** A carbon tax is a key mechanism to accelerate climate action.
    - **Regulatory frameworks:** Regulatory frameworks are needed to accelerate climate action.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## Executive Summary

Funding for emerging technologies that have the potential to deliver energy-related benefits is essential for fostering innovation and scaling cost-effective solutions.

### Key Points

- **Framing Issues**: Affected by the large number of untested strategies, this area requires a clear energy-related strategy.
- **Regulatory Framework**: The Renewable Energy Purchase Agreements (REPA) under Section 2, Article 10(3), and the Green Climate Fund (GCF) guidelines are key regulatory frameworks.
- **Funding Allocation**: GCF has allocated $5.8 billion for emerging technologies from 2014 to 2024, with an average of $670 million per year.
- **Emerging Technologies**: Includes solar, wind, and other renewable energy sources.

### Figure 4: Investment capacity of new renewables, by region (2019-2023)

| Region       | Capacity (GW) |
|--------------|---------------|
| North America | 5.6           |
| Europe       | 7.8           |
| Asia-Pacific  | 8.4           |

### A Government Approach

Governments should consider the following steps to support emerging technologies:

1. **Policy Framework**: Develop a clear policy framework for emerging technologies.
2. **Funding Mechanisms**: Establish funding mechanisms that support innovation and deployment of new technologies.

## Subsection: Integrated Aquatic Subsidy Reduction Models

Integrated aquatic subsidy reduction models, which lower-performing states can adopt, are essential for promoting sustainable development. These models should be integrated into existing policies to ensure effective implementation.

### Key Points

- **Policy Integration**: Policies should integrate subsidy reduction models.
- **Sustainable Development**: Promotes sustainable development by reducing subsidies that harm the environment and economy.

## Subsection: Offshore Wind Energy Technologies

Offshore wind energy technologies are a critical, but underutilized source of financing for emerging technologies. This domestic financing can be a key driver for innovation in the offshore wind sector.

### Key Points

- **Financing**: Domestic financing is essential for driving innovation.
- **Regulatory Framework**: Policies should support the development and deployment of new technologies.
- **Emerging Technologies**: Focus on emerging technologies that cannot be funded through traditional means.

## Conclusion

Governments must take a proactive approach to supporting emerging technologies. This includes developing clear policy frameworks, establishing funding mechanisms, and integrating subsidy reduction models into existing policies. By doing so, governments can promote sustainable development and drive innovation in the energy sector.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.1 PAGE 12

Early-stage, high-impact climate energy investments that provide tangible benefits for local communities and contribute to climate change mitigation are eligible for financing under GCF's private sector window. DFIs participating in the GCF private sector window can finance sheet heating and ice cold project debt, where such projects are not eligible for other sources of financing. The GCF private sector window is designed to provide financing for innovative energy projects that contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation.

DFIs have financed pipelines and fewer projects for emerging climate energy technologies. # Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

### Bank of India (BoI)

Bank of India (BoI), in its role as a climate finance intermediary, has been actively engaged with the Green Climate Fund (GCF) since 2014. BoI has successfully managed climate-related funds, both at the national and international levels. Recently, BoI was accredited by GCF to become an implementing entity (IE). This accreditation enables BoI to access GCF resources directly for climate change mitigation and adaptation projects.

### Confirming GCF clean energy programs in India

GCF's clean energy programs focus on funds from a solar rooftop component. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) supports the implementation of renewable energy, energy efficiency, and sustainable transport projects. GCF's support is aligned with the objectives of the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). GCF financing will be used to accelerate low-carbon and climate-resilient development.

### Enhancing GCF engagement with IFCs

GCF focuses on strengthening its engagement with implementing entities, including financial institutions (IFIs), to enhance their capacity to deliver climate finance. GCF's programs are implemented through NAREDCO, which is a national association of real estate developers in India. NAREDCO has been working closely with GCF to implement various projects.

### Supporting GCF's priorities in India

GCF is actively engaged with the Government of India (GoI) and its implementing entities to support climate action. GCF's engagement with GoI is aligned with GoI's Data Country Programs. GCF will support in enhancing the capacity of GoI to implement climate change projects. The focus areas include renewable energy, energy efficiency, and sustainable transport.

### ADB's continued engagement in India

The Asian Development Bank (ADB) has a long and ongoing energy sector engagement in India. ADB is working with various stakeholders to promote clean energy and climate resilience. ADB's engagement includes financing, technical assistance, and policy dialogue. ADB is also supporting the development of renewable energy projects in India.

### ADB's role in promoting clean energy

ADB has been actively engaged in promoting clean energy in India. ADB's programs are focused on unlocking substantial finance for scaling clean energy and climate resilience. ADB's engagement includes financing, technical assistance, and policy dialogue. ADB is working with various stakeholders to promote renewable energy projects in India.

### ADB's role in promoting climate resilience

ADB has been actively engaged in promoting climate resilience in India. ADB's programs are focused on unlocking substantial finance for scaling clean energy and climate resilience. ADB's engagement includes financing, technical assistance, and policy dialogue. ADB is working with various stakeholders to promote renewable energy projects in India.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

### Supporting Studies: Estimating Carbon Prices in Green CDM Projects and Assessing Risky Technologies to be supported by GCF

#### B.1
The GCF funds are for a short-term intervention to funding upstream studies via enhanced direct access from accredited entities to support the development of carbon pricing mechanisms, including but not exclusively, those that can be used to inform the design of new policies and regulations. The GCF funds will also support the development of methodologies to assess the impact of carbon pricing on different sectors and countries, as well as the potential for using these methodologies to inform policy decisions.

#### B.2
The highest order for the program is to accelerate India's carbon energy transition by reducing greenhouse gas emissions from the power sector. The GCF funds will support the development of a comprehensive strategy to reduce greenhouse gas emissions in the power sector, including the development of new technologies and policies to achieve this goal.

### A.1
Building on this vision, the proposed **Industrial** project can be articulated as follows: **India's CDM has access to concessional finance from GCF to support the deployment of carbon pricing mechanisms.** The project will provide financial incentives for companies to adopt carbon pricing mechanisms and encourage the adoption of low-carbon technologies. The project will also provide technical assistance to help companies implement these mechanisms.

### A.2
To extend this vision, the **Industrial** project is designed by providing finance for **clean energy technologies**. The project will support the deployment of clean energy technologies in India's industrial sector and provide financial incentives for companies to adopt these technologies. The project will also provide technical assistance to help companies implement these technologies.

### B.3
GCF intends to expand its support for green climate portfolios and make investments in a range of innovative, emerging climate technologies that can be deployed at scale. GCF's support for these projects is intended to accelerate the deployment of clean energy technologies and enable countries to achieve their climate goals. The project will also provide financial incentives for companies to adopt these technologies.

### B.4
GCF intends to expand its support for green climate portfolios and make investments in a range of innovative, emerging climate technologies that can be deployed at scale. GCF's support for these projects is intended to accelerate the deployment of clean energy technologies and enable countries to achieve their climate goals. The project will also provide financial incentives for companies to adopt these technologies.

### Output 1.1: GCF's five-year concessional finance to finance emerging clean energy technologies
- Activity 1.1: GCF provides concessional finance to support the deployment of clean energy technologies in India.
- Activity 1.2: GCF provides concessional finance to support the deployment of clean energy technologies in India.

### Output 1.2: Risk-sharing facility (RSF) is established to co-finance investments in CDM projects
- Activity 1.3: GCF provides concessional finance to support the deployment of clean energy technologies in India.

## Table 1: Technology-specific barriers and GCF’s solutions

| Technology                  | Key Barriers                                                                                                                                   | GCF's Solutions                                                                                                                                                           |
|-----------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Renewable Energy**        | High upfront costs, lack of storage and grid integration, weak regulatory environment, limited access to finance, low awareness and capacity. | GCF provides grants and concessional financing for renewable energy projects. It also supports innovative technologies like floating solar and off-grid solutions. |
| **Energy Efficiency**       | High upfront costs, lack of awareness and incentives, insufficient data and information, weak regulatory environment.                            | Concessional capital lowers the cost to early adopters, technical assistance builds capacity, and grants support innovative solutions.                           |
| **Geothermal Energy**       | High exploration risks, lack of data and information, weak regulatory environment.                                                               | GCF supports dedicated $60 million risk sharing facility for geothermal projects. It also provides concessional financing and technical assistance.                |
| **Congestion Relief**      | Lack of investment, high costs, weak regulatory environment.                                                                                       | GCF explores financing models for grid expansion and interconnections. It also supports regional integration initiatives.                                       |
| **Electric Mobility**       | High upfront costs, lack of charging infrastructure, weak regulatory environment.                                                               | GCF explores financing models for EV charging infrastructure and promotes public-private partnerships to support EV adoption.                                    |
| **Decentralized Renewable Energy (DRE)** | High upfront costs, lack of access to finance, weak regulatory environment.                                                                     | GCF improves financing sustainability by reducing DRE project risks through grants and concessional financing.                                                   |

2. **Byrd-Haggard Clauses:** GCF’s instruments may include Byrd-Haggard clauses, ensuring that funds are not used for activities that would increase GHG emissions or undermine climate change mitigation efforts.

3. **Country-level Strategies:** Countries identify interdependencies across sectors, outputs, and outcomes where baseline, additional, and policy achievements are tracked.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

### Executive Summary

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a financial mechanism under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) that helps manage resources to mitigate and adapt to climate change. The GCF aims to promote a paradigm shift towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways by providing funding for projects, programs, policies, and other activities that support these goals.

### B.1 Engaging with Stakeholders

Engaging with stakeholders, financial institutions, and industry representatives ensures that emerging lessons from GCF-funded projects are effectively integrated into the design of future projects. This engagement helps to identify best practices and challenges faced by implementing entities, which can inform the development of new funding strategies.

### B.2 Limited Appetite for Private Sector Investments

One major risk is the limited appetite of private sector investors due to the high-risk perception of emerging clean energy technologies. Many private institutions are hesitant to invest in these projects because they lack the necessary track record and regulatory frameworks. This risk can be mitigated by providing financial incentives, such as concessional loans or equity investments, to attract private capital.

### B.3 Limited Availability of Green Financing Products

Another critical issue is the limited availability of green financing products offered by Development Finance Institutions (DFIs). These institutions should consider developing new financial instruments and expanding their product offerings to meet the growing demand for climate-related investments. This will help to bridge the gap between the supply and demand for green finance.

### B.4 Technical Assistance

Technical assistance is essential in building capacity among implementing entities, particularly those in developing countries. Providing technical support can enhance the effectiveness of GCF-funded projects by ensuring that they are designed and implemented properly.

### B.5 Risk Mitigation Strategies

To mitigate risks associated with green financing, it is crucial to adopt a holistic approach that addresses financial, institutional, and social barriers. This includes implementing risk mitigation strategies such as insurance products, guarantees, and credit enhancement mechanisms.

### B.6 Cross-Cutting Issues

Cross-cutting issues such as gender equality, human rights, and environmental safeguards should be integrated into the design of GCF-funded projects. This ensures that these projects are socially inclusive and environmentally sustainable.

### B.7 Conclusion

In conclusion, the success of the Green Climate Fund depends on its ability to effectively manage resources and engage with stakeholders. By addressing the challenges outlined above, the GCF can enhance its impact and contribute to a more sustainable future.

## Co-benefits

### Mitigation

- **Co-benefit 1:** Improved energy efficiency
- **Co-benefit 2:** Reduced GHG emissions
- **Co-benefit 3:** Enhanced implementation of GCF-financed projects

### Adaptation

- **Co-benefit 1:** Improved resilience to climate change
- **Co-benefit 2:** Reduced vulnerability to climate risks
- **Co-benefit 3:** Enhanced implementation of GCF-financed projects

## B.1 Programme description (max. 250 words, approximately 5 pages)

### Overview

The GCF is a unified funding facility designed to advance low-carbon and climate-resilient development through targeted financing to countries. It leverages technological capabilities from the GCF, combined with country-specific needs and priorities, to support projects that reduce GHG emissions and enhance resilience to climate change. Additionally, the GCF will deploy technical assistance to strengthen project pipelines, address knowledge gaps, and build capacity for effective implementation.

The programme aims to achieve its objectives through a combination of mitigation and adaptation activities. Mitigation activities include reducing GHG emissions from industrial processes, enhancing energy efficiency, and promoting renewable energy sources. Adaptation activities focus on building resilience to climate change impacts, such as improving water management systems, enhancing agricultural practices, and strengthening early warning systems.

### Key design principles

- The programme will be implemented in accordance with the GCF's policies and procedures.
- The programme will be designed to maximize co-benefits for both mitigation and adaptation.
- The programme will prioritize projects that are aligned with national climate change strategies and plans.
- The programme will promote gender equality, social inclusion, and environmental sustainability.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

1. **Programmatic approach**: The Facility aims to programmatic approach to funding, focusing on key development objectives and outcomes, rather than individual projects. This approach enhances efficiency in funding allocation, reduces transaction costs, and supports the development of strategic partnerships with local and regional governments.
2. **Focus areas**: The Facility will focus on energy efficiency, focusing on energy-efficient lighting, cooling, and heating; on energy access, focusing on renewable energy technologies while also supporting climate resilience and adaptation efforts; and on sustainable transport, focusing on low-carbon mobility solutions.
3. **Innovative financing tools**: The Facility aims to use innovative financing tools such as blended finance, guarantees, and credit enhancement instruments to leverage private sector investment in climate action.
4. **Focus on gender-responsive outcomes**: The Facility will strengthen its capacity to integrate gender-responsive outcomes into its operations. This focus is essential for ensuring that the benefits of climate action are equitably distributed among women and men.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V4.1 | PAGE 2

## Component 1: Financing for DFIs to scale up the deployment of emerging clean energy technologies

### 1.0
The GCF will leverage GCF financing for banks and guarantee to complement ADB financing and other solutions. The GCF will provide a combination of financing instruments, including:

- **DFI financing**: to provide blended financing lines of credit for sub-national, regional, and national DFIs for projects in emerging clean energy technologies.
- **Risk Sharing Facility (RSF)**: to enable a Risk Sharing Facility for DFI loans. GCF will leverage RSF with other funding sources.

### Restructuring cost
#### 1.1
The restructuring of technologies for GFF was guided by India's user energy transition goals and the need to address climate change. The restructuring of technologies is essential to ensure that the financing is aligned with the objectives of the GCF.

#### 1.2
The restructuring will include a study and integrated challenges across power, industry, knowledge, and local energy access in the planning process. It will also include the development of a comprehensive plan for the deployment of emerging clean energy technologies. The plan will be developed by leveraging existing resources, including:

- **Solutions**: for the integration of renewable energy sources.
- **Policy**: to support the deployment of emerging clean energy technologies.
- **Financing**: to support the deployment of emerging clean energy technologies.

### 2.0
#### Financing scope
The financing will provide sub-national lines of credit for local banks and guarantee facilities.

##### 2.1
**Powering India's Energy Transition: Projects with access to financing**

- **Rooftop solar**: Rooftop solar energy systems will be financed.
- **Distributed generation**: Distributed generation solutions will be financed.

##### 2.2
**Reducing energy losses in Clean Energy**

- **Electricity**: Electricity is a non-polluting solution.
- **Compressed Biogas**: Compressed biogas is a clean and renewable source of energy.

### Prioritize Flexible Energy Systems for Rural and Urban Areas

- **Output 1.2**: Develop and promote flexible energy systems to support rural electrification and improved livelihoods in urban areas.

### GCF Readiness Support

As part of the project design, consultations were held with several DFIs based on supporting clean energy and related climate change mitigation projects. These consultations included a review of the DFI's pipeline of projects, their track record in financing the uptake of critical sectors and technologies from the perspective of DFIs, and lessons learned from the implementation of GCF-funded projects. This will help to identify potential DFIs for potential collaboration under GCF's Readiness Support.

  - Total number of beneficiaries: 1,242,000

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

The outcomes of the analysis will help to discuss how GHGs can be identified and optimal use of technology for greenhouse gas emissions reduction can be achieved. The proposed project will work with GHG accounting processes to identify and implement possible measures to reduce GHG emissions from the energy sector. This will include assessing the impact of using renewable energy sources, improving energy efficiency, and promoting cleaner technologies. Cross-cutting issues such as gender equality, youth employment, and environmental sustainability will be addressed.

### Table: Proposed funding items for GCF funds under Output 1

| Category                  | Sub-Outputs | Cost (USD) | Total (USD) |
|---------------------------|-------------|-----------|------------|
| Energy Efficiency         | 1.1         | 50,000    | 200,000    |
| Renewable Energy          | 1.2         | 70,000    | 300,000    |
| Clean Cooking             | 1.3         | 40,000    | 150,000    |

### Funding criteria

The following criteria will be applicable to all sub-projects under Output 1:

- **Eligible technologies**: Sub-projects should include energy efficiency measures such as lighting, heating, and cooling. Renewable energy projects including photovoltaic (PV) systems, wind turbines, and small hydro power plants.
- **Energy technologies**: Sub-programmes will target its imaging clean energy technologies aligned with India's energy security objectives.
- **Renew the Cooking energy system that contains renewable energy with energy storage** to improve the cooking energy system.
- **Improving energy efficiency in buildings**: This includes improving building insulation, reducing heat gains, and promoting efficient lighting and appliances.
- **Promote energy efficiency in industry and transport**: This will include measures such as improving industrial processes to reduce emissions, promoting fuel-efficient vehicles, and supporting public transport.
- **Underserved areas**: Sub-projects to be designed to target at least 30% of funding for cities in the country with less than 50,000 people. The use of renewable energy will be defined by each beneficiary's needs and the availability of local resources.
- **Gender equality**: At least 40% of the sub-projects should be led by women or include gender-specific activities.
- **GCF financing share**: At least 50% of the sub-projects should be financed from GCF funds, with the remaining 50% coming from other sources.
- **Leverage**: At least 80% co-financing from ADB and/or other co-financers is targeted for each sub-project.

### Implementation

The project will be implemented over a period of five years. The first year will focus on planning, capacity building, and procurement. The subsequent four years will focus on implementation, monitoring, and evaluation. The project will be managed by a team of experts in energy efficiency, renewable energy, and climate change mitigation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V.1 PAGE 8

Total grant resources for ESF proposal. The GCF will be provided alongside a US$7 million guarantee (ACB) loan to the same REPF projects that are funded by the RSF.

## B

The RSF scheme includes the following components:
- Business case: This is based on the RSF project unit cost and the expected IRR. The business case is used as a basis for feasibility studies, including undertaking benefit-cost analysis.
- Coverage: The premiums will be set at a level that ensures affordability (covering both premium and interest on a loan).
- Tenure and duration: The RSF will have a 10-year term. This long tenure accommodates the high upfront costs of REPF projects, which are repaid over time.
- Overarching RSF duration: The RSF will be operated for 25 years—the first five years focusing on active generation loans and portfolio building. Subsequent years (years six to ten) focus on refinancing and balance sheet management.
- Leverage: The RSF is set with a leverage of 3, meaning the US$60 million guarantee can mobilize up to US$180 million in REPF projects.

### Additional information (ESF PDF)

The RSF scheme includes credit insurance and risk sharing via a concessional loan from the MDB. This arrangement will be funded by guaranteed RSF loans if each borrower’s revenue or a performance-based loan. The mechanism allows for the RSF to provide additional support to the REPF projects.

### C

The RSF design builds on precedents from existing MDB-financed guarantee schemes in other countries, including the World Bank’s Global Environment Facility (GEF) and National Adaptation Funds. It also draws on the experience of the Climate Investment Funds’ Clean Technology Fund (CTF), which provides support for renewable energy projects through a combination of grants and loans.

### D

The RSF will be implemented by the ESF, with the REPF Manager responsible for managing the RSF portfolio. The REPF Manager will be supported by the ESF’s technical team to ensure that the RSF is effectively managed.

### E

The RSF mechanics will be as follows: The GCF loans for the RSF will be provided to the REPF Manager, which will in turn provide a loan to the REPF projects. The REPF Manager will then repay the GCF loans from the premiums paid by the REPF projects.

### F

The RSF will be implemented in the ESF and will be managed by the REPF Manager. The REPF Manager will be responsible for managing the RSF portfolio, including the disbursement of funds to the REPF projects.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## Guarantee Mechanism

Guarantee mechanism: GCF funds all new interest and funded on the project and leverage from IFS. Risk Management Unit (RMU) will manage the guarantee portfolio. The RMU will be responsible for managing the guarantee portfolio, including guarantee selection and risk assessment. Annual guarantees fees will be charged to the DFI to cover the operational costs of the RMU. The RMU will be accountable to the GCF to ensure that the fees charged by the DFI are commensurate with the level of risk and is affordable by MDB borrowers.

### Guarantee Fee Structure

Guarantee fee structure: 0.5% of the total amount of guarantee issued for the first five years, and 1% thereafter. The RMU will be responsible for collecting the fees from the DFI. The RMU will also be responsible for ensuring that the fees are paid in a timely manner.

## Figure E: Fund Flow Arrangement in Risk-Sharing Facility

### Structure of the Risk Sharing Facility and Related Fund Flows

- **GCF**
  - Directly funds the facility
  - Funds are used to cover the guarantee fees and other operational costs

- **ADB**
  - Provides additional funding to the facility
  - Funds are used to cover the guarantee fees and other operational costs

- **DFI (IFS)**
  - Provides leverage by issuing guarantees
  - Funds are used to cover the guarantee fees and other operational costs

#### Sub-components of DFI (IFS)
1. **Guarantee Fees**
   - Collected from DFIs
   - Used to cover operational costs

2. **Leverage**
   - Provided by DFIs
   - Used to increase funding capacity

3. **Risk Management**
   - Managed by RMU
   - Ensures proper risk assessment and management

## Example of GCF EEAF at Work

Example: EEAF at work is a mechanism that allows the GCF to leverage additional resources from other sources, such as DFIs. This can help to increase the overall funding available for climate change mitigation and adaptation projects.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

### Implementing arrangements

1. The GCF Management Agency (GMA) will manage the GCF accounts and disburse in line with fiduciary guidelines. RPs are to be selected based on their capacity to be not just a financial intermediary but GOM (i.e., the entities created to include managing, implementing, and reporting on climate change projects). The GMA will also ensure that the RPs have the necessary systems and processes in place for effective management of GCF funds. Prioritizing PFIs, we also seek to show inclusion of a broad range of actors, including women, youth, indigenous peoples, and local governments, over the GCF funding cycle. In accordance with the guidance documents, TA approved under Output 2.2 will support the GOM and RPs, with this line being managed by the National Designated Authority.

### In case of default

PFIs will follow recovery procedures and may take a claim to the GCF Manager. A loan accordion clause will be included in all loan agreements. The financial intermediary guarantees are not recourse to the GOM or the RPs. The GOM is responsible for ensuring that the PFIs (which bear 50% of the risk) and their sub-recipients comply with the recovery procedures.

### The following terms and conditions will apply to the RPs

- The RPs will be required to provide a detailed list of all projects, including the expected disbursement dates.
- The GOM will ensure that the RPs are in compliance with the requirements set out in paragraph 101.
- The GOM will ensure that the RPs are in compliance with the requirements set out in paragraph 102.
- The GOM will ensure that the RPs are in compliance with the requirements set out in paragraph 103.
- The GOM will ensure that the RPs are in compliance with the requirements set out in paragraph 104.

| Category | Number of Loans | Currency (USD) | Tenor (years) | Grace Period (years) |
|----------|-----------------|---------------|--------------|----------------------|
| Category A | 10              | USD           | 7            | 2                    |

### Specific criteria applied for the PFI vehicle

- The GOM will ensure that the RPs are selected based on their track record and experience in managing climate finance.
- The GOM will ensure that the RPs have a strong financial position.
- The GOM will ensure that the RPs have a good reputation in the market.
- The GOM will ensure that the RPs have a good track record of delivering projects on time and within budget.
- The GOM will ensure that the RPs have a good track record of managing climate finance schemes / funds or issuing guarantees.

### Eligible investments

- The GOM will ensure that the PFI vehicle is selected based on its ability to deliver projects in line with GCF requirements.
- The GOM will ensure that the PFI vehicle has a strong financial position.
- The GOM will ensure that the PFI vehicle has a good reputation in the market.
- The GOM will ensure that the PFI vehicle has a good track record of delivering projects on time and within budget.
- The GOM will ensure that the PFI vehicle has a good track record of managing climate finance schemes / funds or issuing guarantees.

### Additional considerations

The GOM will ensure that the PFI vehicle is selected based on its ability to deliver projects in line with GCF requirements. The GOM will also ensure that the PFI vehicle has a strong financial position and a good reputation in the market. The GOM will also ensure that the PFI vehicle has a good track record of delivering projects on time and within budget, as well as managing climate finance schemes / funds or issuing guarantees.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

This activity aims to enhance the technical and institutional capacity of DFPs receiving GCF support under Output 3.1. It will strengthen the institutional and technical capacities of at least 5 DFPs through targeted technical assistance, including training, coaching, mentoring, and knowledge management. This output will enable DFPs to effectively implement their projects and contribute to achieving climate change mitigation and adaptation results. Through focused assistance and tailored training, DFPs will be equipped to design, develop, and implement robust and effective projects that contribute to achieving sustainable development outcomes.

### Objectives

- **Objective 1:** Strengthen the capacity of DFPs to effectively manage GCF support.
- **Objective 2:** Enhance the technical expertise of DFPs in project management.
- **Objective 3:** Improve the ability of DFPs to monitor and evaluate projects.
- **Objective 4:** Build DFP staff capacity to effectively engage with GCF processes.

### Activities

1. **Conduct needs assessments and develop tailored assistance packages for each DFP supported by Output 3.1.**
   - Assess capacity and institutional needs, identify areas of improvement, and develop targeted assistance plans.
   - Provide technical support to address identified gaps in project management, monitoring, and evaluation.

2. **Design technical assistance support packages.**
   - Develop tailored support packages for each DFP based on their specific needs.
   - Collaborate with Project Preparation Teams (PPTs) to ensure alignment with GCF requirements and objectives.

3. **Strengthen knowledge management systems.**
   - Establish a centralized repository of best practices, lessons learned, and success stories.
   - Facilitate peer-to-peer learning and networking among DFPs.

4. **Build DFP staff capacity.**
   - Conduct training programs to enhance project management skills.
   - Provide mentoring and coaching support to build technical expertise.

5. **Develop knowledge products and training programs.**
   - Create comprehensive guides, toolkits, and manuals on best practices in GCF project management.
   - Develop training materials and conduct workshops to build capacity among DFP staff.

6. **Promote gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) considerations.**
   - Integrate GESI into all aspects of the technical assistance program.
   - Ensure that projects are designed and implemented with a focus on inclusivity and equity.

7. **Strengthen environmental and social safeguards implementation.**
   - Provide support to DFPs in meeting GCF environmental and social safeguard requirements.
   - Conduct training and capacity-building activities to enhance understanding of safeguards.

8. **Enhance monitoring, evaluation, and reporting (MER) systems.**
   - Support DFPs in developing robust MER systems.
   - Provide technical assistance to improve data collection, analysis, and reporting capabilities.

9. **Promote climate resilience and adaptation.**
   - Integrate climate resilience into project design and implementation.
   - Provide support to DFPs in mainstreaming adaptation measures into their projects.

10. **Foster partnerships and collaboration.**
    - Facilitate partnerships between DFPs and other stakeholders.
    - Promote knowledge sharing and collaboration among DFPs and with GCF Secretariat.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Output 2.3: Enhance green jobs and sustainable livelihoods through green financing for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) with financial services in a new fund under development with PFF and other financial institutions.

### Objective:
To enhance the capacity of SMEs to access finance and improve their competitiveness by providing them with access to affordable, long-term financing from commercial banks and microfinance institutions. This will enable SMEs to invest in green technologies and practices that reduce greenhouse gas emissions and increase energy efficiency.

### Activities:

1. **Output 2.3.1: Enhance the capacity of financial institutions to provide green loans.**
   - Develop guidelines for assessing the creditworthiness of SMEs applying for green loans.
   - Provide training on green financing products and services to bank staff.
   - Establish partnerships with microfinance institutions to promote green lending.

2. **Output 2.3.2: Enhance the capacity of SMEs to access green finance.**
   - Develop a database of SMEs that are interested in accessing green finance.
   - Provide training on green financing products and services to SME owners and managers.
   - Facilitate access to affordable, long-term financing for SMEs through partnerships with commercial banks and microfinance institutions.

3. **Output 2.3.3: Enhance the capacity of financial institutions to provide green loans.**
   - Develop guidelines for assessing the creditworthiness of SMEs applying for green loans.
   - Provide training on green financing products and services to bank staff.
   - Establish partnerships with microfinance institutions to promote green lending.

### Results:

- Strengthen local financial sector capacity through targeted technical assistance and policy dialogue.
- Facilitate access to affordable, long-term financing for SMEs through partnerships with commercial banks and microfinance institutions.
- Enhance the competitiveness of SMEs by providing them with access to affordable, long-term financing.

### Indicators:

1. Number of financial institutions trained on green lending products and services.
2. Number of SMEs accessing green finance.
3. Increase in the number of green loans issued by commercial banks and microfinance institutions.
4. Increase in the number of SMEs investing in green technologies and practices.

### Annex 1: Detailed activities, timelines, and budget allocations for Output 2.3.

- **Activity 1:** Develop guidelines for assessing creditworthiness of SMEs applying for green loans.
  - Timeline: Q1-Q2
  - Budget: $50,000

- **Activity 2:** Provide training on green financing products and services to bank staff.
  - Timeline: Q3-Q4
  - Budget: $75,000

- **Activity 3:** Establish partnerships with microfinance institutions to promote green lending.
  - Timeline: Ongoing
  - Budget: $25,000

- **Activity 4:** Develop a database of SMEs interested in accessing green finance.
  - Timeline: Q1-Q2
  - Budget: $30,000

- **Activity 5:** Provide training on green financing products and services to SME owners and managers.

  - Budget: $60,000

- **Activity 6:** Facilitate access to affordable, long-term financing for SMEs through partnerships with commercial banks and microfinance institutions.
  - Timeline: Ongoing
  - Budget: $150,000

# Green Climate Fund Proposal V.1

## B

### Executive Summary

The proposal will involve stakeholders to enhance the inclusion of energy policies and regulations for the benefit of the country. It will also support the development of a national policy on climate change and the implementation of the Paris Agreement. The GCF (Green Climate Fund) is a fund created in 2010 for advancing the goals set out in the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The GCF helps developing countries to limit or reduce their greenhouse gas emissions and adapt to climate change.

### Project Planning

The initiative project proposal for the Program is presented in the table below. This option was used to design the project. The project will be implemented by the Ministry of Environment, Forestry and Climate Change (MOEFCC). The MOEFCC has informed the program design. They remain interested and subject to changes. Projects may be adjusted as necessary.

| Project | Responsibilities and Activities |
|---------|----------------------------------|
| **Green Fuel Development Project** | **Responsible:** MOEFCC <br> **Activities:** Acquisition of compressed biogas (CBG) plants, installation of CBG plants in 100 villages, production of 500 tons of CBG per year. |
| **National Policy on Biogas (2018)** | **Responsible:** MOEFCC <br> **Activities:** Development and implementation of a National Policy on Biogas, development of a biogas policy framework, and promotion of biogas as an alternative fuel source. |

### Project Details

The initiative aligns with India's National Policy on Biogas (2018) and the SAFTA (South Asia Free Trade Agreement), which aims to promote the use of CBG plants. The project will focus at least 500 tons of CBG per year.

The project will be implemented by MOEFCC, with support from IFC (International Finance Corporation). The project will involve the installation and operation of CBG plants in 100 villages across India. The project aims to promote the use of CBG as an alternative fuel source for vehicles and other applications.

### Additional Information

The project will be implemented over a period of five years, with an initial phase of one year for planning and preparation. The project will involve the installation of CBG plants in 100 villages across India, with a target production of 500 tons of CBG per year. The project aims to promote the use of CBG as an alternative fuel source for vehicles and other applications.

The project will be implemented by MOEFCC, with support from IFC (International Finance Corporation). The project will involve the installation and operation of CBG plants in 100 villages across India. The project aims to promote the use of CBG as an alternative fuel source for vehicles and other applications.

# B

## Overview

Following the GCF Board approval, ADB and GCF ML signed the RMA, after a joint-specific page agreement (the "Yurked Activity Agreement" or the "FAA"). The FAA will enable, among others, the selection of projects and the provision of funds to the GCF through the ML. This will allow the GCF to disburse funds to ADB for the implementation of the GCF-funded projects and to ensure that the GCF funds are used in accordance with the GCF policies and procedures. The FAA also enables the GCF to monitor the use of its funds by ADB, including through regular reporting and audits.

Using GCF funds will be based on criteria outlined in this proposal where we have included detailed Program Implementation Guidelines to be developed following FAO's experiences and will be used throughout for GCF funds implementation. The guidelines will include the preparation of project documents, procurement, environmental and social safeguards, financial management, and reporting arrangements.

During the implementation period, between the entry into effect date FAA for its budget, GCF resources will be made available to ADB for the implementation of projects. This will enable ADB to prepare and implement activities in accordance with the approved project documents and procurement procedures, including through the use of ADB's internal policies and procedures.

### One difference and subproject preparation

One difference between this proposal and the FAA is the use of and ADB's Country Programming Document (CPD). The implementation of the investment capacity and readiness of the CPD will be included in the preparation of the project documents. This will enable GCF to be assured that the funds are used for the intended purpose, and terms and conditions of the funding are met.

The difference between this proposal and the FAA is that the use of ADB's Country Programming Document (CPD) will be included in the preparation of the project documents. The CPD will include the implementation capacity and readiness of the CPD to be included in the preparation of the project documents. This will enable GCF to be assured that the funds are used for the intended purpose, and terms and conditions of the funding are met.

On subprojects, we will be given which will allow us to prepare subproject documents on the basis of the FAA. The use of ADB's Country Programming Document (CPD) will be included in the preparation of the project documents. This will enable GCF to be assured that the funds are used for the intended purpose, and terms and conditions of the funding are met.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## Overview of Legal Arrangements

### Components

#### Component 1 (RNF)

A conditional grant using GCF envelope resources is a selected ECSP RNF Manager to establish the RNF as part of a subprogram. ADB, as AE, will enter into a loan agreement with the RNF Manager (RNF). The RNF Manager will be responsible for managing and implementing the RNF subprogram. The RNF Manager will be accountable to the AE for the use of the conditional grant and resources through regular reporting. The RNF Manager will be subject to the same audit arrangements as the GCF envelope.

The DFID will be responsible for establishing the RNF under a separate trust (subject to other arrangements). The RNF Manager will be accountable to the DFID for the use of RNF subprogram funds and will report to the AE on the use of conditional grant resources. The RNF Manager will be subject to the same audit arrangements as the GCF envelope.

All relevant governance is brought PRIs for these projects include conditions for environmental and social safeguards, the RNF Manager will sign a governance agreement with the PRIs and provide reporting for progress towards meeting these conditions. The RNF Manager will also provide reports on environmental and social safeguards to the AE.

### 110

"For Component 3, ABR will apply for the EIE for technical assistance grant resources. When seeking a RE, ADR or other funding sources, ABR will ensure that the funding is sufficient to cover all applicable AMA and FAA requirements.

#### Program management and governance

##### 111

In its capacity as an ADB, ABR will administer funds, grants and technical assistance from GCF under the IGF. The following are the key elements of program management:

- Monitor GCF funding in our GCF fiduciary accounts and remittances as set out in the FAA and AMA, to provide assurance that all disbursements from ADB's own resources and other GCF administrative funding is appropriately applied, reporting the same to the GCF.
- Ensure that all GCF funds are used for the purposes intended by the GCF, including the use of technical assistance in accordance with the FAA and AMA.
- Manage AMA and FAA requirements.

##### 112

The following arrangements will be put in place to guide program implementation (see Figure B):

- Establish a GCF Program Management Unit (PMU) within ADB's existing organizational structure, comprising technical and administrative staff. The PMU will be responsible for the overall management of the GCF program, including the preparation of reports required by the FAA and applicable AMA policies. The PMU will include representatives from ADB's finance, procurement, legal, environment, energy, climate change, and other relevant units.

- An AGP program management unit (PMU) will be established, with a dedicated fund manager, and supported by an advisory committee comprising representatives of the GCF, ADB, and the executing agencies. The PMU will be responsible for the overall implementation of the AGP, including the preparation of reports required by the FAA and applicable AMA policies.

- For non-industrialized DFs, ABR will establish a PMU to oversee implementation of the subprograms. This PMU will include local staff and technical experts from ADB's regional departments. The PMU will be responsible for overall program management, including the preparation of reports required by the FAA. The PMU will also ensure that all subprograms are implemented in accordance with the FAA and applicable AMA policies.

- For the RICF subprogram, the RICF Manager will manage implementation of the subprogram, including the preparation of reports required by the FAA and applicable AMA policies.

1. **RFP** is launched to the target need and identified deep decarbonization areas in India's economy by introducing innovative financing mechanisms. The RFP will be open to all eligible applicants who can provide clean energy projects that contribute to play a central role in achieving India's NDC and long-term strategy. The RFP will also help to identify new clean energy technologies, where the clean energy market is underdeveloped against the global standards. The remaining funds will be used to support STI and DI activities. The RFP will be launched in the first year (during the RFTC cycle), which can extend to 12 years, to complete the average of new projects that are expected to be implemented over the next five years.

3. **GCF** funds will enable RFPs to support SMEs and innovative clean energy technologies by providing concessional financing and long-term capital, de-risking investments in high-impact but underfunded technologies, and strengthening financial systems and markets for clean energy projects. The GCF funding will also help to leverage additional private sector investment.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

### 150% support is critical for scaling emerging clean energy technologies

RTC projects in India face considerable financial and structural challenges. While managing debt and equity financing is a major hurdle, the lack of structured project finance remains a significant barrier to growth. This gap prevents the deployment of renewable energy resources at scale. The Indian government has recognized this challenge and has introduced various initiatives to address it. These include the National Investment and Infrastructure Fund (NIIF), which aims to mobilize private capital for infrastructure development, and the Green Energy Corridor project, which seeks to integrate renewable energy into the national grid.

### 135

Green hydrogen, though pivotal in decarbonizing hard-to-abate sectors like steel and chemicals, remains a nascent technology. The current cost of green hydrogen production is approximately $2-6/kg, making it uncompetitive with fossil fuel alternatives. However, advancements in electrolyzer technology and renewable energy integration could reduce this cost to $1.50/kg by 2030.

### 128

The CBD sector currently promotes M&ME developments. Secure project structure and financial barriers limit the growth of clean energy projects. The high cost of capital hinders the development of clean energy projects, particularly in emerging markets like India's SATAY region. The lack of structured financing options makes it difficult for developers to secure funding for their projects.

### 125

Electric mobility, especially buses and two-wheelers in urban and peri-urban India, faces a different set of challenges. The high cost of electric vehicles (EVs) and the lack of charging infrastructure are significant barriers to adoption. However, initiatives like FAME II aim to address these issues by providing subsidies for EV purchases and setting up charging infrastructure.

### 120

The CBD sector also faces legal and regulatory hurdles that hinder its growth. The lack of clear regulations and policies makes it difficult for developers to navigate the complex landscape of clean energy projects. Additionally, the lack of standardized contracts and financing structures further complicates the process.

### 115

Finally, off-grid solar-powered irrigation pumps offer one of the most economically viable clean energy solutions. These systems are simple, reliable, and cost-effective, making them an attractive option for farmers in remote areas.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## B

### R & E: Energy (approx. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

132. GCF has been designed with a strong emphasis on long-term sustainability and a clear structure that ensures the effective use of resources to achieve its objectives. It will support the development of robust institutions, capacity building, and enhance private investment in climate-friendly technologies.

133. GCF promotes sustainable financing mechanisms through innovative financial instruments with multiple benefits. These include green bonds, blended finance, and other innovative financing mechanisms. Through these tools, GCF will be able to leverage additional resources from the public and private sectors for climate action.

134. To ensure effective implementation, GCF has adopted a robust governance structure that includes an independent secretariat, a board, and a range of committees. This structure ensures transparency, accountability, and effectiveness in the use of funds.

135. Parallel to geographic expansion, GCF also aims to mainstream green funding practices with its accredited entities. This will involve developing partnerships with financial institutions, development banks, and other relevant actors. GCF’s support will enable these entities to develop and implement climate finance projects that are aligned with national priorities. This systemic change ensures that the impact of GCF support extends well beyond the project level.

136. The beauty’s financing solutions reinforce the long-term sustainability. Concessional loans provided through GCF will be blended with ADB’s financing, including the wealthier countries’ cost of capital and extending loan tenors to 20 years. This will allow for a significant reduction in debt service costs. For example, a $1 billion concessional loan from GCF could reduce the annual debt service by approximately $50 million.

137. As mentioned earlier, the use of concessional finance will lower off financial constraints faced by developing countries. This will enable them to invest more in climate action and sustainable development. Enhanced cooperation with DFIs can further amplify these benefits.

138. It is important that GCF’s support is tailored to local needs and priorities. This includes providing technical assistance, capacity building, policy guidance, and enhancing the enabling environment for green finance. The use of concessional capital will enhance the leverage of additional resources and promote sustainable growth.

139. In sum, GCF’s role is pivotal in mobilizing—tens of billions of financial assets and funds to climate action. GCF support is catalytic; it is performed by unlocking financing flows, expanding financing to underserved regions, and promoting innovative financing mechanisms that can attract additional resources.

Under Component 2, the program will provide technical assistance to SMEs to prepare climate risk assessments tools, develop standardized project evaluation methodologies, and improve sectoral understanding of climate risks and opportunities. # EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section presents the performance of the project against the investment criteria as set out in the GCP.

## 1. Invested capital (NPV) weeks, approximately 1 year

### 1B - GOFF design is expected to deliver long-term stability by reducing investments & lowering new energy charges, enabling access to renewable fuels and strengthening the financial position of the company. GOFF will be implemented in two phases:

- **Phase I:** Modification of existing plant
- **Phase II:** New build

### 1A - GOFF is expected to modulate USD 18 million in close-ended investments and facilitate the deployment of the following technologies:

- **RCT:** Replacement of 30 MW of conventional steam turbine with 47 MW of RCT (replacement capacity)
- **Decarbonized Boiler Feedstock:** Decarbonization of ~6,000 SHP site-owned boilers (passes a ~1,500 SHP power plant)
- **Compressed Biogas:** 344 GJ/year, 11.5 PJ/year, 82 TWh/year, and 1.3 PJ/year, reducing CO₂ emissions by ~600,000 tons
- **Green Hydrogen:** 451 MW of electrolyzer capacity, enabling the production of ~3,000 tonnes of green hydrogen

The benefit to a green economy under GCF is expected to generate approximately 130,284 jobs across the country. This will be achieved through the development of renewable energy projects, which will be based on solar implementation, particularly in farming, domestic use, and small-scale industries.

### 147

GCF project aims to reduce GHG emissions from the burning of biomass fuels, thereby substantially contributing to Kenya's development efforts. To put this into perspective, wood energy remains one of the most widely used sources of energy for cooking and heating purposes in rural areas. The project will focus on improving the efficiency of traditional cookstoves by replacing them with improved cookstoves (ICS) that use less fuel and emit fewer GHGs.

### 148

Renewable energy is a key component of Kenya's development strategy, which aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and improve access to clean energy. Additionally, it contributes to the country's efforts to achieve its commitments under the Paris Agreement on climate change. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 7 (affordable and clean energy) and SDG 13 (climate action).

### Section 3: Implementation Strategy

#### Objective 1
The GEF focuses on a targeted approach to mitigate climate change. This includes reducing greenhouse gas emissions, promoting renewable energy sources, and enhancing carbon sinks. The GEF will support projects that aim to:

- Reduce methane emissions from agriculture.
- Promote the use of clean cooking fuels.
- Enhance forest conservation efforts.

#### Objective 2
Further, the program analyzes a modest expansion in undernourished geographies, financing 15,000 electric 3-wheeler rickshaws and 5,000 solar-powered microgrids. This will enhance the quality of life for rural communities by providing access to clean energy sources.

#### Objective 3
GEF targets its aim at reducing greenhouse gas emissions through targeted investments in renewable energy projects. Key players in this initiative include:

- National governments.
- Private sector partners.
- Non-governmental organizations (NGOs).

### Section 4: Financial Projections

Indicative GEFs are central to scaling up climate-change investments, particularly in regions where conventional financing mechanisms are limited. The GEF aims to:

- Provide strategic partnerships to drive a financial strategy.
- Develop innovative capital to leverage new investment opportunities.

#### Financing Highlights
- **Highlight 1:** Over the next five years, an anticipated $2 billion will be allocated for renewable energy projects.
- **Highlight 2:** This includes $500 million earmarked for small-scale renewable energy initiatives in developing countries.
- **Highlight 3:** An additional $750 million is targeted towards enhancing climate resilience and adaptation efforts.

| **Social**              | - GCF will support a just transition to a low-emission and climate-resilient future. It aims to empower women, improve agricultural productivity, and boost local wealth through sustainable land management and climate-smart agriculture. The program also supports the development of climate-resilient infrastructure and services in urban areas. |
| **Knowledge**           | - GCF will support the development of knowledge and capacity building for climate change adaptation and mitigation. # Needs of the country

The country’s energy policy is guided by its NDCs and aims to reduce emissions intensity by 35% and carbon emission intensity by 45% by 2030, as submitted to the UNFCCC. Achieving these targets requires a systemic change of policies and practices in the energy sector. The country has to deal with diesel, and electricity access is still an issue in some areas. Double-digit tariffs on renewable energy equipment hinder the development of renewables within the 2030 horizon.

## Needs of the country

### Needs of the country

The country’s energy policy is guided by its NDCs and aims to reduce emissions intensity by 35% and carbon emission intensity by 45% by 2030, as submitted to the UNFCCC. Achieving these targets requires a systemic change of policies and practices in the energy sector. The country has to deal with diesel, and electricity access is still an issue in some areas. Double-digit tariffs on renewable energy equipment hinder the development of renewables within the 2030 horizon.

### Needs of DFI

DFI (Development Finance Institutions) can play a key role in addressing the needs identified above by providing:

- **Long-term financing:** DFI can provide long-term financing to support infrastructure projects.
- **Risk mitigation:** DFI can help mitigate risks associated with infrastructure investments, making them more attractive to private sector investors.

## D

### Technical assistance groups GFs with focus on climate risk analysis, green audit, ESG compliance and reporting

#### 108

- **India** has demonstrated a strong political commitment, backed up by policies and regulations that are underlying the development of its **Green Climate Fund (GCF)**.
- India has made a commitment to reduce its emissions intensity by one-third by 2030 compared to 2005 levels. This can be achieved through substantial increases in the share of non-fossil fuel-based power, an increase in forest cover by 2020 and a CO2 emission reduction by 33-35% by 2030.
- **India's National Action Plan on Climate Change** and the **National Mission for Enhanced Energy Efficiency (NMEEE)** are two of the most important initiatives that have been undertaken to address climate change.

### India's National Action Plan on Climate Change and the National Missions

#### 107

The Government has launched **National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC)** on 30th June 2008. The NAPCC consists of eight missions:

- **National Solar Mission**
- **National Mission for Enhanced Energy Efficiency (NMEEE)**
- **National Mission for a Green India**
- **National Water Mission**

#### 108

India updated **NAPCC** under the Paris Agreement reflects the country's commitment to addressing climate change. The NAPCC has been revised to include new missions such as:

- **Climate Smart Agriculture**
- **Himalayan Adaptation, Water and Livelihoods Program (HAWLP)**
- **Intensified Mission for a Green India**

### Additional policies/ policy instruments aimed at working this beauty (fisheries and agriculture) sector

#### RTC Renewable Energy (RE)

- The Government has announced the **Renewable Purchase Obligation (RPO)** which mandates utilities to purchase a certain percentage of electricity from renewable sources.
- The National Tariff Policy 2016 has been amended to include provisions for RTC Power. This policy was issued in 2003, enabling RTC power.

### Battery Storage

**Page 60**
