# The Utility of Solar Energy: The National Policy on Energy Storage Systems (NPES)

The National Policy on Energy Storage Systems (NPES), issued by the Department of Energy (DOE) in October 2018, aims to accelerate the development and deployment of energy storage systems. This policy is designed to address the challenges associated with the integration of renewable energy sources into the electric grid.

## Green Hydrogen

Green hydrogen refers to hydrogen produced through electrolysis using renewable energy sources such as solar or wind power. The National Renewable Energy Laboratory (NREL) has developed various policy interventions and programs to support the production, usage, and export of green hydrogen. These include the National Mission on Transformative Mobility and Battery Storage Systems.

### Green Hydrogen Mission

The Green Hydrogen Mission was launched in 2021 as part of the National Hydrogen Mission aimed at creating an ecosystem for the production, usage, and export of green hydrogen and its derivatives. The mission aims to produce green hydrogen at a competitive price and promote its usage in sectors such as transport, industry, and agriculture.

### Comprehensive Hydrogen

The Hydrogen Energy Vision 2030 is part of the National Policy on Hydrogen to 2030. It aims to develop an ecosystem for the production, storage, and distribution of hydrogen. The policy also includes measures to promote the use of hydrogen in various sectors such as transportation, industry, and energy.

### Off-grid Solar Water Pumps

The program is implemented jointly by the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy (MNRE) and the Ministry of Rural Development. It aims to provide solar-powered water pumps to farmers in remote areas where grid electricity is not available. The program also provides training and maintenance support to ensure the efficient operation of the pumps.

### Solar Fodder for Rural Areas

The program aims to provide solar-powered fodder units to rural farmers. These units use solar energy to produce fodder for livestock, which can be used as animal feed. The program is implemented jointly by the MNRE and the Ministry of Agriculture.

# Green Climate Fund Proposal

## Solar Power Grid Storage

The Government of India has approved a capital investment scheme for the development of solar storage. This comprises the installation of grid-linked projects totaling 10 GW of solar power with integrated storage. The project aims to promote the use of renewable energy and reduce dependence on fossil fuels.

### Key Features
- **Technology**: The project will utilize advanced energy storage technologies.
- **Funding**: The project is funded by multi-lateral and national funds, along with energy efficiency technologies.

## MOU Signing

MOUs have been signed between India and countries such as the United Kingdom, France, and Germany. These agreements aim to promote cooperation in areas like renewable energy, climate change, and sustainable development. The MOUs include:
- **United Kingdom**: Agreement on Climate Change.
- **France**: Agreement on Renewable Energy.
- **Germany**: Agreement on Climate Change.

## International Meetings

Several international meetings were conducted with the Secretary of the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy (MNRE) and the Joint Secretary. These meetings focused on cooperation in renewable energy, climate change, and sustainable development. The discussions included:
- **United Kingdom**: Agreement on Climate Change.
- **France**: Agreement on Renewable Energy.
- **Germany**: Agreement on Climate Change.

## Green Climate Fund Meetings

On October 8, 2021, MOB organized a virtual meeting with the Ministry of India's Finance Secretary. The discussions focused on the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the need for India to become a member. The meetings included:
- **NAMAMD**: Agreement on Climate Change.
- **REDA**: Agreement on Renewable Energy.
- **SORA**: Agreement on Sustainable Development.

## GCF Meetings

During the past March-May 2021, ADB has had regular consultations with each of the DFI's to utilize their expertise in climate change and renewable energy. The meetings included:
- **NAMAMD**: Agreement on Climate Change.
- **REDA**: Agreement on Renewable Energy.

## Future Plans

Future plans include providing technical assistance to promote the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and other climate-related funds, especially in India.

### Objectives
- **Efficiency**: Improve energy efficiency in all sectors.
- **Affordability**: Ensure affordability of renewable energy technologies.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## Additionally and Effectiveness of Cross-cutting Levers and Guarantees

### Deeply systemic, de-risking measures such as parity reforms, regulatory enhancements, and financial deepening are essential to accelerate clean energy investments.

Financial deepening includes both conventional loans and guarantees, plus (potentially) the use of innovative instruments like green bonds. These measures can help reduce the cost of capital for clean energy projects by lowering the risk premiums paid by investors. This is particularly important in emerging markets where the risks are higher and the regulatory environment is less developed.

The effectiveness of these instruments depends on the ability of the regulatory framework to ensure that they are used appropriately, including managing risks and reducing concentrations, which guarantees additional risks related to regulatory concerns. However, recent evidence suggests that these measures can be effective in reducing risk premiums and encouraging private investment and public-private partnerships.

![Figure 8: Deeply effective systemic instruments for financing clean energy projects](image_url)

### Common pool funds are essential to managing natural and financial resources for emerging clean energy investments.

These funds can help manage the risks associated with emerging technologies, such as carbon capture and storage (CCS), and nuclear power. This is particularly important in countries where the regulatory environment is less developed, and where there may be a lack of experience in managing these types of projects. These funds can also help to reduce the cost of capital for clean energy projects by providing a pool of resources that can be used to finance new technologies.

### Guarantees affect financing for CDRs

These include mitigating accelerated risks of DFIs and PIFs due to increased exposure to climate change, which can lead to higher costs. These guarantees are essential to managing the risks associated with these types of projects, including the potential for catastrophic losses.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## Cost Efficiency of Off and On-Grid Energy for Money

### 18f
With an estimated cost per tonne of CO2 abated of USD 55 (including the entire investment cycle of USD 230m repaid to be raised by GCF, the proposed investment is a competitive compounded to gain the highest possible return on investment). The proposed investment will be implemented in countries with high potential for renewable energy development. The cost of CO2 abated is estimated at USD 55 per tonne of CO2 abated. The proposed investment will be implemented in countries with high potential for renewable energy development.

### 18g
The cost of clean energy is still high, but for key emerging technologies targeted by the GCF, such as concentrated solar power (CSP) and onshore wind, the levelized cost of electricity has fallen dramatically. For example, in India, a recent auction for 750 MW of CSP achieved a record-low price of INR 2.97/kWh ($0.043/kWh), which is lower than the average retail tariff of INR 6.44/kWh. Similarly, in the United States, the cost of onshore wind has fallen by more than 50% over the past decade, achieving an average price of $20/MWh. These trends suggest that the technology costs will continue to fall and that the potential for renewable energy development is significant.

![Graph](image_url)

### 18h
The facility will support the GCF to finance sustainable projects that are economically viable but that require additional financing and are not currently being supported by the financial sector. The facility will provide a total of USD 500 million (USD 250 million from the GCF and USD 250 million from other sources). The facility will be implemented in countries with high potential for renewable energy development.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Objective

The objective "defined mechanism provided by DFIs will include training on incentive for climate change related project and knowledge of potential projects.

### Key Projects

- The key projects that are eligible under this proposal include:
  - Renewable energy projects.
  - Energy efficiency projects.
  - Sustainable transport projects.
  - Sustainable water management projects.
  - Sustainable agriculture projects.
  - Other projects approved by the GCF.

## Eligibility Criteria

The eligibility criteria for this proposal will be based on the following:

- The project must be aligned with the objectives of the GCF.
- The project must have a positive impact on climate change mitigation and adaptation.
- The project must be implemented in accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards.

## Financial Support

The financial support provided under this proposal will include:

- A grant component to cover pre-investment costs.
- A concessional financing component to cover capital expenditure.
- A technical assistance component to support capacity building and knowledge sharing.

### Concessional Financing Scheme

The GCF's concessional financing scheme will provide the following:

- A fixed interest rate of 0.25% per annum for projects in Least Developed Countries (LDCs).
- A fixed interest rate of 1.00% per annum for projects in other developing countries.
- A grace period of up to five years.

### Technical Assistance

The technical assistance component will provide the following:

- Capacity building and training activities.
- Knowledge sharing and networking events.
- Support for the preparation of project proposals.

## Table of Financial Terms

| Project Type                | FIRR  | WACC (GT Only) | WACC (GF) |
|-----------------------------|-------|----------------|-----------|
| Development Sector PV       | 0.1   | 15.1           | 8.3       |
| Energy Efficiency           | 0.2   | 14.7           | 9.6       |
| CCS (INDIRECT)              | 0.3   | 14.2           | 9.3       |
| Off-Grid Solar PV           | 0.4   | 15.8           | 9.2       |

## Conclusion

The GCF's concessional financing mechanism enhances the financial viability of key clean energy projects in developing countries. This includes renewable energy, energy efficiency, and off-grid solar power projects.

On the other hand, despite stronger financial viability, E. McKinsey, CCSG, and Off-Grid Solar Purposes remain underfunded due to the perception that they are high-risk investments.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## PAGE 18

"To ensure these Raze barriers, IFFF combines concessional finance with risk-sharing mechanisms and guarantees to facilitate private sector investment in renewable energy projects. This includes leveraging blended finance by providing and/or borrowing risk funds, with every dollar in guarantees making up to six times in investments. The IFFF will also support the development of local capital markets through innovative financial instruments such as green bonds and other securitization products. These experiences with the instrument (like Article 3, EPA, and Annex 2, Financing Study, Appendix 4) is essential for large-scale investments as well as on rural and smallholder agriculture, forestry, fisheries, renewable energy, and sustainable infrastructure.

### The IFFF Facility

The IFFF facility will not use public capital but will catalyze future private sector participation by leveraging private capital through blended finance. This includes the following:

- **Risk Mitigation**: Providing guarantees to reduce risks for private investors.
- **Guarantees**: Providing guarantees to reduce risks for private investors.
- **Subsidies**: Providing subsidies to lower costs and increase affordability.

The IFFF will also provide technical assistance to support the development of local capital markets. This includes:

- **Capacity Building**: Training local financial institutions in green finance.
- **Policy Support**: Supporting governments in developing policies that promote green investment.
- **Market Development**: Facilitating market development through pilot projects and demonstration initiatives.

### Financial Structure

The IFFF will be structured as a trust fund with an endowment of $10 million. This will be augmented with AICS pledges on contributions from other members, private foundations, and governments. The trust fund will be managed by an independent board of directors, not through the secretariat. Funds raised through the IFFF will be used to support projects that are consistent with the objectives of the IFFF.

### Governance

The governance structure of the IFFF will include representatives from:

- **AICS**: Providing technical assistance and policy support.
- **Private Sector**: Representing private investors.
- **Governments**: Providing policy support and market development.

The board of directors will be responsible for overseeing the implementation of the IFFF and ensuring that it is consistent with the objectives of the IFFF. The board will also ensure that the IFFF is transparent, accountable, and effective in achieving its objectives."

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V4.0 | PAGE 82

## Sustainability

### What are GCFs?

GCFs are on-the-ground finance mechanisms to support developing countries in mitigating and adapting climate change. They aim to go beyond traditional development-making, ESG-investment frameworks.

- Permanent integration of climate finance into national budgets.
- Integrating climate into the design of decision-making, ESG-investment frameworks.

## Loss

By the end of GCF, GCFs will have embedded student non-shareholder capital into sustainable green funding. The original purpose is to support the transition towards a low-carbon economy and to promote sustainable development. This includes:

- Integrating climate finance with existing ESG and climate risk assessment methods.
- Enhancing market confidence, sustainability, and clean energy investment.

## GCF will strengthen UNDP capacity to assist and implement climate change mitigation and adaptation projects.

- Accelerated project implementation.
- Private sector engagement in green lending practices.
- Enhanced collaboration mechanisms, including partnerships with the private sector.
- Improved access to finance for climate action.
- Strengthened capacity of developing countries to access international climate finance.

| **Middle (5 years)** | **Final (10 years)** | **Middle (5 years)** | **Final (10 years)** |
| - Climate Resilience (CR) | - Climate Resilience (CR) | 265 million tonnes of CO2e (4.38 million tCO2e/year) | Final: 12.7 billion ICOS (1.95 billion tCO2e/year) |
| - Sustainable Forest Management (SFM) | - Sustainable Forest Management (SFM) | 60 million tonnes of CO2e (10 million tCO2e/year) | Final: 3.8 billion ICOS (633.33 million tCO2e/year) |
| **Total** | **Total** | **325 million tonnes of CO2e (5.42 million tCO2e/year)** | **Final: 16.5 billion ICOS (2.75 billion tCO2e/year)** |

### Assumptions / Notes

- The net benefit might decrease during the first five years due to the lag effect.
- The baseline period is set at the end of the implementation period.
- For the initiative (100 million investment), assume the project size target at the end of the total budget period which is half of the implementation period.
- The baseline period is set at the end of the implementation period.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V4.1 | PAGE 8

## Overview

- **Objective:** To ensure that climate finance is used effectively and efficiently.
- **Approach:** Utilizing existing screening models. Additionally, leveraging Green Climate Fund (GCF) applications for climate finance to identify underutilized funds due to lack of access or affordability.

## GCF Funding Opportunities

| Category                          | Details                                                                                   |
|-----------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Sector**                        | - Solar<br>- Hydroelectric<br>- Wind<br>- Geothermal<br>- Low Emission Development (LED) |
| **Technology**                    | - Solar PV<br>- Solar Heating<br>- Wind Turbines<br>- Small Hydropower<br>- LED Lighting |
| **Geographical Coverage**         | Developing countries, including 48 LDCs and 32 SIDS.                                    |
| **Number of Projects**            | 17,000 projects enhancing climate resilience and reducing emissions.                   |
| **GCF Funding**                   | USD 5 billion (USD 1.9-3.6 billion for mitigation and USD 1.3-1.4 billion for adaptation).|
| **GCF Board Approval**            | USD 2.8 billion approved in 2017.                                                      |
| **GCF Accredited Entities**       | - National Designated Authorities (NDAs)<br>- Implementing Entities (IEs)                |

## IFFT Proposal

- **Objective:** To involve private sector investments and leverage the expertise of international organizations to promote commercial adoption of climate-friendly technologies.
- **Key Components:**
  - Solar
  - Hydroelectric
  - Wind
  - Geothermal
  - LED Lighting
- **Implementation Strategy:**
  - Develop a detailed plan for each technology.
  - Establish partnerships with relevant stakeholders.
  - Create awareness campaigns to promote the use of these technologies.

## Funding Allocation

- **GCF Board:** USD 1.5 billion (USD 700 million for mitigation and USD 800 million for adaptation).
- **Other Funds:** USD 3.5 billion from various international funding sources.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V4.0 | PAGE 8

## High-level Goals and Key Performance Indicators (KPIs)

- **Consolidate T. Diasee** to reduce emissions from the cement industry by implementing best available technologies (BAT) for carbon capture, utilization, and storage (CCUS).
- **High-level KPIs and KPIs** include:
  - Reducing CO2 emissions by 30% compared to business-as-usual.
  - Increasing energy efficiency by 15% through the implementation of energy management systems.
  - Achieving a 95% utilization rate for carbon capture technologies.

## Climate Policy & Development Strategy

### Objectives in the GCF context
- Enhance climate resilience and reduce vulnerability to climate change impacts.
- Promote low-emission development pathways.
- Strengthen climate policy, planning, and implementation processes.

### GCFs' Role
- Provide technical assistance for climate policy development.
- Support capacity building and knowledge management.
- Facilitate access to finance for climate action.

## Climate Finance

### GFs' Role
- Enhance climate resilience and reduce vulnerability to climate change impacts.
- Promote low-emission development pathways.
- Strengthen climate policy, planning, and implementation processes.

### GFs' Role in Knowledge Sharing and Capacity Building
- Facilitate knowledge sharing among GCF stakeholders.
- Support capacity building for effective climate action.
- Enhance understanding of climate finance mechanisms.

## Green Climate Fund (GCF)

### GCF's Role in Knowledge Sharing and Capacity Building
- Facilitate knowledge sharing among GCF stakeholders.
- Support capacity building for effective climate action.
- Enhance understanding of climate finance mechanisms.

### GCF's Role in Financial Support
- Provide financial support to developing countries.
- Enhance climate resilience and reduce vulnerability to climate change impacts.
- Promote low-emission development pathways.

## E: Monitoring, Reporting, and Evaluation (M&E) Framework

### 108. Purpose of the M&E Plan

The M&E Plan for the GCF (and for GEF Accreditation) aims to support the Program Management Unit (PMU) and GCF's M&E measures for Project Preparation and Implementation. The M&E Plan will ensure that the PMU is able to monitor and evaluate the impact of the project, including its financial and environmental impacts.

### 109. M&E Structures, Data Management, and Reporting

The M&E framework ensures the collection and submission of reports to the GCF. The PMU will be responsible for collecting data from accredited entities and reporting it to the GCF. The PMU will also review DFI-submitted reports, providing feedback on quality and completeness. DFIs are encouraged to submit their own reports directly to the PMU.

- **Monitoring Tools**: Data collection tools are designed to facilitate a monitoring plan that is aligned with the PMUs and the M&E framework.
- **Data Management**: Data management will be conducted in consultation with the PMUs and the M&E framework.
- **Organic Monitoring**: Organic monitoring will be integrated into the project's operations.

### Policy Trials

Support for GCF's accreditation and internal management system, including green funding and accreditation processes, and gender-responsive policies.

Organic monitoring will be integrated into the project's operations. Data collection tools are designed to facilitate a monitoring plan that is aligned with the PMUs and the M&E framework.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.A.E | PAGE #E

The GCF Readiness Programme (RPs) will be implemented by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) in partnership with accredited entities to support developing countries and regions in their preparation for, and implementation of, GCF-funded projects. The same monitoring tools used by the GCF will be applied to RPs.

- **Quality and Annual Reporting:** PPFAs/MOUs will submit progress reports to DFIs based on agreed targets. Reports will be stored in the DFI's MIS system and shared with GCF. The GCF will provide feedback to PPFAs/MOUs for compliance.
  - **Reporting:** Annual reports will be submitted by partners (governments, NGOs, private sector) jointly and annually
- **PMU Constitution:** The PMU will review and align reports, ensuring alignment with Global Framework indicators and compliance with GCF requirements. The PMU will also ensure that the reports are aligned with the GCF's results management framework.
  - **Reporting:** Annual reports will be submitted by partners (governments, NGOs, private sector) jointly and annually
- **Knowledge Management:** The PMU will maintain a repository of lessons learned, best practices, and other knowledge products. The PMU will also facilitate the sharing of experiences and lessons learned among GCF accredited entities.
  - **Reporting:** Annual reports will be submitted by partners (governments, NGOs, private sector) jointly and annually

The PMU will support the PAFAs in their reporting to the GCF against targets in the logical framework indicators. Implementation challenges, lessons learned, and best practices will be documented and shared with the GCF.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.3 Risk factors and mitigation measures (Page 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1 – Forex risk

#### Category: Financial

##### Description:
108. **FOR and ABS** is not subject to the FRR, a condition for the funding of GGF. GFF will receive EUR funding from the ECB. The EUR funding will be provided in the form of a fixed rate loan with a 3-year maturity. The interest rate on the loan is fixed at 0.25% p.a., which is significantly lower than the current market rates for EUR funding.

#### Mitigation Measures:

109. **Mitigation Measure(1)**:
    - **Category**: Financial
    - **Probability**: Low
    - **Impact**: Low

    **Description**:
    The risk factor is mitigated by the fixed rate loan provided to GGF by the ECB, which is subject to the FRR.

# Selected Risk Factor 3 - Risk to potential to receive climate finance

## Category: Operational

### Description:
251. The GIF has an equal implementation of climate finance, green economics, climate risks and what is needed for implementing adaptation and mitigation actions (projects).

### Mitigation (Measures):
252. Curriculum for the faculty, AIDS has been in close and continuous dialogue with the relevant banks, which have maintained contact with both the government and the private sector. Furthermore, the Faculty includes a number of students from the local community who are involved in various income-generating activities, thereby contributing to enhancing the economic knowledge and skills that the bank offers to structure the economy.

## Selected Risk Factor 4 - Lack of implementation of policy & regulatory framework

### Category: Operational

### Description:
253. There is a lack of comprehensive policy and regulatory framework. This limits and guides the sectors in implementing climate change adaptation measures, thereby contributing to the vulnerability of the sectors. Although there have not been detailed policies or laws relating to climate change.

### Mitigation (Measures):
254. The GIF will partner with the relevant ministries and national banks to ensure that the policy dialogue is implemented.

## Selected Risk Factor 5 - Inconsistent implementation of policy & regulatory framework

### Category: Operational

### Description:
255. There is an inconsistent regulatory framework which can vary from implementing national policy at the local level for climate change adaptation measures. This has led to a lack of consistency in the implementation of policies and regulations, thereby contributing to the vulnerability of the sectors.

### Mitigation (Measures):
256. The GIF will partner with the relevant ministries and national banks to ensure that the policy dialogue is implemented.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
|----------|-------------|--------|

### Description
This risk refers to the probability that GCF financing through GFF to the DFIs results in money laundering, normal financing or prohibited practices.

### Mitigation Measures
- **Risk Level:** Low
- **Mitigation Strategy:** As GCF funds will be provided strategically (e.g., financing from ADB, ADI, Integrity Principles and associated due diligence) only at the project level, which will also trigger the risk. Furthermore, host DFI’s will be required to comply with the same requirements as GCF, including anti-money laundering (AML) and know your customer (KYC) as a minimum.

## GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### S.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### 21.
The GCF’s support approach to Environmental and Social (ES) assessment is guided by the Green Climate Fund’s Environmental and Social Safeguards Policy (GCF ESP), which aims to ensure that projects funded by the GCF are designed and implemented in a manner that minimizes or mitigates adverse environmental and social impacts. The GCF ESP aligns with the principles of the International Finance Corporation’s Performance Standards on Environmental and Social Sustainability, which are designed to avoid or minimize adverse environmental and social impacts and mitigate, or where possible, remedy them.

#### 212.
GCF will finance direct-financed projects through GCF Focal Points (FPs) in accordance with the approved policies. This includes the use of the Environmental and Social Risk Management Framework (ESRM). The GCF ESP is designed to ensure that projects are implemented in a manner consistent with the principles of the IFC Performance Standards on Environmental and Social Sustainability. The GCF will require FPs to conduct an ES risk assessment for each project, which will be used to determine the level of due diligence required.

## 0.1 Gender measurement and action plan (page 38, women's empowerment) (page)

### 216.
Women are often stakeholders in India, as well as in many other countries, who have been excluded from energy and resource management decisions. This is due to the fact that they are not considered as decision-makers or household heads. However, this is changing rapidly with the growing recognition of women's roles in managing natural resources and their contributions to sustainable development. Women play a key role in ensuring food security and nutrition for their families, and they have been recognized as important agents of change in promoting sustainable development. The GCF can support projects that empower women and promote gender equality.

### 217.
Achieving the Document of rights. Article 15(3) of the Constitution of India provides for equal opportunities to all citizens regardless of their sex, race, caste, religion or place of birth. However, in practice, these rights are often denied to women due to social and cultural barriers. The GCF can support projects that promote gender equality and empower women by providing them with access to education, health care, and economic opportunities.

### 218.
REDD+ Gender Guidelines (page 39). The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) has developed a set of gender guidelines for REDD+ projects. These guidelines provide practical advice on how to ensure that REDD+ projects are designed and implemented in a way that is gender-responsive and promotes gender equality.

### 3.3 Financial management and procurement plan, 100 words, approximately 1 page
The financial management and procurement plan will include an overview of the project's financial accounting, disbursement procedures, and procurement processes. The plan will be developed in accordance with the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the GCF's Financial Management and Accountability Procedures which will be included in the project's procurement documentation. The plan will also include a detailed budget breakdown, including all costs associated with the project, such as personnel costs, equipment costs, and other related expenses. The plan will also outline the procedures for financial reporting and auditing, including the frequency of reports and the format of the reports.

### 223. Financial Management and Audit
Details of every grant's financial accounting, disbursement procedures, procurement processes, and audit requirements are provided in the GCF's Financial Management and Accountability Procedures which will be included in the project's procurement documentation. The plan will also include a detailed budget breakdown, including all costs associated with the project, such as personnel costs, equipment costs, and other related expenses. The plan will also outline the procedures for financial reporting and auditing, including the frequency of reports and the format of the reports.

### 224. Integrity and Anti-Corruption Policy
AOB will conduct integrity due diligence on DFIs, the members of their Board of Directors, and their management teams, in accordance with AOB's Anti-Corruption Policy (1987). As a result, the project will be conducted in accordance with the highest standards of integrity.

### 225. Procurement
Procuring services is subject to execution of the school assistance grant for academic year 2023-24. The procurement process will follow the GCF's Financial Management and Accountability Procedures which will be included in the project's procurement documentation. The plan will also include a detailed budget breakdown, including all costs associated with the project, such as personnel costs, equipment costs, and other related expenses. The plan will also outline the procedures for financial reporting and auditing, including the frequency of reports and the format of the reports.

#### 3.4.1 Professional conduct
The proposed entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed by the GCF as it deems necessary in accordance with the GCF's Access to Information Policy (2015). The entity also confirms that any information provided by the DFIs or their affiliates is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the proposed entity will not use such information for any purpose other than that for which it was disclosed and will take all reasonable steps to ensure that such information is kept confidential.

- All data for members are used by the GCF when the confidentiality provisions are met accordingly.
- Members will be required to sign a non-disclosure agreement (NDA) with the GCF.
- The entity confirms that it will comply with the GCF's Access to Information Policy (2015).

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the ten copies above. If containing confidential information, please ensure that the confidentiality provisions are met accordingly.

Our Reference No.: __________

We refer to the programme title “ADB-GCF Green Finance Facility (GFF) for India” which is included in the funding proposal submitted by ADB to the GCF on 17 August 2024.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEF&CC), Government of India.

Pursuant to GCF decision 9.065, the content of which we acknowledge, has requested us to confirm that no objection exists to the programme as included in the funding proposal.

By confirming our no-objection, it is also implied that:

(a) The programme is consistent with the country’s priorities as set out in the funding proposal;

(b) The programme is consistent with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards; the programme is included in the country programme in conformity with relevant national law and regulations.

We are confident that a no-objection response for accommodating no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal will be provided by the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEF&CC), Government of India.

We also confirm that no no-objection applies if it imposes or activates its participation with the above GFF programme.

Please note that this no-objection does not preclude the programme from being made publicly available on the GCF website.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FF271

- **Proposed name:** India Green Finance Facility (IGFF)
- **Accredited entity:** Asian Development Bank
- **Country:** The Republic of India
- **Programme size:** Large

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths
The India Green Finance Facility (IGFF) programme focuses on accelerating sustainable development by providing long-term financing to support green transport, agriculture, and renewable energy projects. It aims to address issues such as:

- Decarbonizing power generation.
- Reducing greenhouse gas emissions from industrial processes.
- Promoting the use of clean technologies in the manufacturing sector.

The programme supports the growth of large-scale renewable energy projects by providing financing for on-grid and off-grid renewable energy market borrowers. It also aims to develop innovative financial instruments, such as:

- Green bonds.
- Green loans.
- Green guarantees.

These instruments are designed to support the development of sustainable infrastructure, which is crucial for achieving the goals of the Paris Agreement.

### Points of concern
Private sector participation in the programme is limited to the Risk Sharing Facility (RSF), which provides partial credit guarantees. There is a need to enhance private sector engagement and ensure that the programme effectively addresses the challenges faced by small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in accessing green finance.

There is also a possibility that the private sector might lack interest in certain sectors as the market may not be ready for an overwhelming entry of new projects.

# The IFF will also provide technical support to institutionalize green and gender-responsive lending practices and strengthen the capacity of DFI’s to design the IFFs in partnership with other development finance institutions (DFIs). This includes providing technical assistance to DFIs to enhance their capacity for gender-responsive lending, including gender analysis tools, gender-responsive credit risk management frameworks, and gender-responsive environmental and social safeguards. The IFF will also provide technical support to DFIs to strengthen their capacity to integrate climate change considerations into their lending practices.

The program aims to expand the IFF’s reach by providing technical assistance to 10 additional DFI members in the next three years. This includes a focus on DFI members that are new to the IFF, with a particular emphasis on those in Africa and Asia. The program will also provide technical support to DFIs to enhance their capacity to integrate climate change considerations into their lending practices.

In terms of environmental and social safeguards (ESS), this component has been integrated into environmental and social ESS risk management (component 1). The review by the Secretariat will be conducted in accordance with the IFF’s Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS).

## Component-by-component analysis

### Outcome 1: Enhanced technical assistance to DFIs for green lending (expected cost: USD 380,000 million; GGF and IDA 77% funded)

This component will provide the IFF with technical assistance to support DFI members in developing and implementing green lending policies and procedures. This includes providing technical assistance to DFI members to develop and implement green lending policies and procedures, as well as providing training and capacity building for DFI staff on green lending.

### Outcome 2: Enhanced capacity building for ESS, climate risk management, RESSI, and gender-responsive lending (expected cost: USD 380,167 million; GGF and IDA 77% funded)

This component will provide the IFF with technical assistance to support DFI members in developing and implementing enhanced environmental and social safeguards that have the capacity of financial institutions to address climate change risks. This includes providing technical assistance to DFI members to develop and implement enhanced environmental and social safeguards, as well as providing training and capacity building for DFI staff on climate risk management, RESSI, gender-responsive lending, and gender-responsive environmental and social safeguards.

The component will also provide technical support to DFIs to enhance their capacity to integrate climate change considerations into their lending practices. This includes providing technical assistance to DFI members to develop and implement enhanced environmental and social safeguards that have the capacity of financial institutions to address climate change risks. The component will also provide training and capacity building for DFI staff on climate risk management, RESSI, gender-responsive lending, and gender-responsive environmental and social safeguards.

The component aims to catalyze systemic change in India’s financial sector by enhancing the capacity of DFIs to integrate climate change considerations into their lending practices. This includes providing technical assistance to DFI members to develop and implement enhanced environmental and social safeguards that have the capacity of financial institutions to address climate change risks. The component will also provide training and capacity building for DFI staff on climate risk management, RESSI, gender-responsive lending, and gender-responsive environmental and social safeguards.

# Green Climate Fund

## Financial instruments, including incentives and scalable finance for the country’s clean energy transition

### Project title: Renewable Energy Financing and Investment Framework (REFIF) (ID: 102_113_001)

#### Objective
Promote implementation and monitoring of renewable energy projects through innovative financial instruments. This will enhance the country's capacity to implement and monitor investment and expenditure processes for climate change mitigation and adaptation.

### Gender, environmental and social management framework

An environmental and social management framework (ESMF) and a gender action plan (GAP) are developed to ensure that the project is implemented in an environmentally and socially sustainable manner. The ESMF will include measures to address potential adverse impacts on biodiversity, water resources, and other environmental concerns.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

REFIF is a transformative initiative designed to unlock large-scale investment in clean energy technologies. It targets energy access and climate resilience by promoting renewable energy projects that reduce greenhouse gas emissions. REFIF will support the development of renewable energy projects, including solar, wind, hydro, and geothermal power. The project aims to generate 15 GW of new renewable energy capacity over its lifetime, which is expected to reduce carbon dioxide emissions by approximately 20 million tons per year.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
**Scale: High**

REFIF promotes a paradigm shift in the way renewable energy projects are financed and implemented. It targets the development of innovative financial instruments that will provide deep and sustainable reductions in greenhouse gas emissions. The project aims to support the development of renewable energy solutions, which are critical for further scaling up and widespread adoption by providing a stable power supply.

## Role of women in clean energy transitions

Additionally, the project will create over 139,000 green jobs and better rural resilience by expanding DRE technologies, thus catalyzing economic growth.

### GCF's investment, economy, and social impact benefits

GCF’s investments, economy, and social impact benefits include:

- **Long-term economic growth**: The project will reduce greenhouse gas emissions, improve air quality, and strengthen water security.
- **Energy access**: It will support energy access through reliable and affordable electricity, reducing the need for fossil fuels.
- **Women's empowerment**: Women’s participation in clean energy projects is expected to increase, leading to improved livelihoods.

### Needs of the recipient

The project aims to address the needs of women in the world’s least developed countries. Energy access is still heavily dependent on fuel-based, with an ever-increasing energy demand that it cannot meet.

Women face significant barriers when trying to access clean energy technologies and services. The project will support women’s empowerment by providing them with the skills and resources they need to succeed in the clean energy sector.

# India is heavily indebted, and hence is unable to fund the investments required for an effective energy transition on its own. The country is currently bringing in assets foreign investors are interested in, such as renewable energy projects. Other activities include loans and investments, but it cannot afford foreign financing without compromising its national climate action and energy security and its potential to contribute to global efforts to mitigate climate change. India needs support from the international community to help the public and private sectors and will help to foster long-term collaboration with other countries.

## 3.5 Country ownership

India is a country that is well-equipped to be able to effectively manage the climate transition. The government has shown its commitment to the country's GEF priorities and objectives, and the government has demonstrated its ability to implement projects in the country. The GEF plays an important role in supporting the country's efforts.

The program is aligned with the stated NDCs (nationally determined contributions), national policies, and strategies. It is also consistent with the GEF strategic plan and the GEF-7 programming document. Furthermore, it aligns with the objectives of the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

The architecture of the program consists of NABARD, REC, and IREDA, which are all national institutions that have been involved in the implementation of climate change projects for many years. The program will build on these institutions' strengths and experience to ensure effective implementation.

## 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

In terms of efficiency, the GEF is designed to maximize economic and climate benefits while minimizing transaction costs. It aims to support countries in their efforts to achieve national climate objectives and contribute to global environmental benefits. The program will be implemented through a combination of grants and low-interest loans from various sources, including multilateral development banks and bilateral donors.

The GEF supports the country's efforts to address climate change by providing financial resources for projects that are consistent with the country's priorities and needs. The GEF also provides technical assistance to help countries implement their projects effectively. In addition, the GEF helps countries to build capacity and develop human resources to support their climate change efforts.

The program is designed to be efficient and effective in achieving its objectives. It will be implemented through a combination of grants and low-interest loans from various sources, including multilateral development banks and bilateral donors.

# Green Climate Fund

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

#### c. IGFT background

The IGF will benefit from GCF's catalogue of environmental and social safeguards (ESS) that are designed to avoid or minimize adverse environmental and/or social impacts. These safeguards include, among others:

- **Climate-related risks:** The IGF will be exposed to climate-related risks, including those associated with the lack of renewable energy development that might increase greenhouse gas emissions.
- **Energy access:** The IGF will benefit from GCF's support for energy access projects, which are designed to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and promote sustainable development.
- **Gender equality:** The IGF will benefit from GCF's support for gender equality initiatives, which aim to ensure that women and girls have equal access to opportunities and resources.

The IGF's basic safeguards include or qualify under the ESS and include:

1. **Environmental and social impact assessments (ESIA):** The IGF will conduct ESIA to identify potential environmental and social impacts of its projects.
2. **Free, prior, and informed consent (FPIC):** The IGF will obtain FPIC from affected communities before implementing any project that might affect their rights or interests.
3. **Gender equality:** The IGF will promote gender equality in all its activities and ensure that women and girls have equal access to opportunities and resources.

The safeguards are designed to ensure that the IGF's approved projects are consistent with GCF's ESS, and that they do not cause adverse environmental or social impacts. The safeguards also aim to ensure that the IGF's projects contribute to sustainable development and poverty reduction.

#### d. Compliance with ESS standards

The compliance with GCF's ESS standards is a prerequisite for receiving the GCF's financial support. The IGF will comply with these standards in all its activities, including:

- **Environmental and social impact assessments (ESIA):** The IGF will conduct ESIA to identify potential environmental and social impacts of its projects.
- **Free, prior, and informed consent (FPIC):** The IGF will obtain FPIC from affected communities before implementing any project that might affect their rights or interests.
- **Gender equality:** The IGF will promote gender equality in all its activities and ensure that women and girls have equal access to opportunities and resources.

The compliance with GCF's ESS standards is a key component of the IGF's strategy for achieving sustainable development and poverty reduction. The IGF will work closely with the GCF to ensure that it complies with these standards and that its projects contribute to sustainable development and poverty reduction.

# ESS-2: Labour and working conditions

Major subjects under the programme would include issues of workers' rights. The ESS programme integrating issues related to ESS-2 would include:

- Working hours (including overtime) and safety at work.
- Working conditions in the informal sector, including domestic labour.
- Working conditions for women and children.
- Working conditions in the agricultural sector.

# ESS-3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The programme is designed to promote resource efficiency and pollution prevention. It would include:

- The protection of water and the air, soil, power and other downstream resources.
- The promotion of sustainable consumption and production patterns (e.g., energy efficiency).
- The reduction of waste (e.g., solid waste, hazardous waste) and the development of recycling systems.
- The protection of biodiversity and ecosystems.

# ESS-4: Gender equality, labour and security

The programme is designed to promote gender equality in the workplace. It would include:

- The promotion of equal pay for work of equal value.
- The elimination of discrimination based on sex.
- The promotion of women's rights and their full participation in all aspects of life.

- The protection of land rights and the promotion of sustainable land management practices.

- The promotion of the rights of indigenous peoples and their participation in decision-making processes.

# Implementation

During IGT implementation, stakeholders will be engaged through structured communication capacity building workshops and collaborative partnerships. The Government of Canada (GoC) will provide financial support to provinces and territories to undertake risk assessments, and pilot financial products. Private sector investment will also be encouraged.

Governments will collaborate with Indigenous organizations to ensure that Indigenous peoples' unique needs are addressed in the IGT implementation. GoC security and health committees will consider inputs, co-design programs, and develop partnerships with Indigenous communities. The IGT program will establish regular opportunities for partnership between federal, provincial/territorial, and Indigenous governments to share information on IGT-related outputs and discuss risks, impacts, and management issues in the context of the IGT.

## Gender-based violence (GBV) response

The IGT response to GBV is a vital part of community and civil security engagement. Each subproject funded under the IGT will require a GBV risk assessment and an action plan to address identified risks. The GBV response will be based on the evidence-based practices and the global design features of an effective GBV response, including comprehensive screening, referral pathways, and support for survivors. The GBV response will be enhanced by real-time data collection, affected population needs assessments, and community engagement. The GBV response will also include a focus on addressing the root causes of gender-based violence.

### Scales and standards

The IGT will establish scales and standards to ensure that all subprojects funded under the IGT meet minimum requirements for GBV risk assessment and response. The IGT will provide guidance, training, and technical assistance to support the implementation of these standards. The IGT will also work with partners to develop a comprehensive approach to addressing gender-based violence.

## Gender policy

The ALP has provided a gender analysis and action plan and therefore aligns with the IGT Gender Policy. Women's and girls' face significant systemic and structural challenges that limit their ability to participate in security, peacebuilding, and recovery efforts. These challenges are exacerbated by deeply rooted social norms, discrimination, and unequal access to resources and opportunities. The ALP will support women's participation in all aspects of the IGT implementation process, including through gender-responsive procurement, recruitment, and retention policies.

The ALP will also promote equal pay for equal work and ensure that women have equitable access to economic opportunities. The ALP will support initiatives that address the root causes of gender inequality and promote women's empowerment.

# Green Energy Fund

## Women's Participation in India's Renewable Energy Sector

Numbers in the workforce, women’s participation in India’s renewable energy sector is extremely low (17%), with significant underrepresentation in technical and leadership roles. The sector has a gender gap of 40% compared to the national average of 34%. Women face several barriers such as societal biases, safety concerns, limited vocational training, wage gaps, and lack of mentorship opportunities. Additionally, intersectional discrimination affects their wages, job security, working conditions, and access to benefits.

The sector also lacks gender-responsive policies and programs that address women’s needs and ensure their participation and leadership in the workforce. The Global Alliance for Green and Gender Action (GAGGA) is a consortium of organizations working together to strengthen, amplify, and scale up women’s participation in green economies. GAGGA aims to promote gender equality, climate justice, and sustainable development by supporting women-led initiatives that address environmental challenges.

The program focuses on empowering women and girls in the renewable energy sector through capacity building, advocacy, and community engagement. The initiative also supports women’s leadership in decision-making processes and promotes gender-responsive policies and practices. GAGGA works with partners to ensure that women have access to the resources they need to succeed in the renewable energy sector.

The program has trained 2,000 female entrepreneurs in green business development, engaging 300 local communities in sustainable energy initiatives. The program also supports women’s cooperatives and provides training and mentorship opportunities for women entrepreneurs. GAGGA is committed to promoting gender equality and empowering women in the renewable energy sector.

- **a.** The structural components of the overall program are medium-high risk.
  - **i.** The program has a high level of complexity, requiring multiple stakeholders to work together effectively.
  - **ii.** The program is subject to external factors that could impact its success.

#### Program-Specific Execution Risks (Medium-High Risk)

- **b.** The AEI is the LE for this project. The AEI has committed $50 public funds and grants from other sources, with a total of $100 million in funding.
  - **i.** The program is selected by the DEI in accordance with the eligibility criteria for the Development Impact Bond (DIB).
  - **ii.** The program is subject to external factors that could impact its success.

#### Program-Specific Execution Risks (Medium-High Risk)

- **c.** Servicing Credit Risk: The borrower of the GEF funds amounting to USD 12 million for a period of five years.
  - **i.** The borrower has a high level of credit risk due to its exposure to the renewable energy sector.
  - **ii.** The program is subject to external factors that could impact its success.

# AI/AN Introduction to its traditional AML/CFT Framework

AI/AN introduces its traditional AML/CFT framework to provide the foundations for regulation requirements. This includes enhanced scrutiny for foreign, known or suspected money launderers and terrorists, and increased reporting of suspicious transactions such as the abuse of Anorexia and bulimia, illegal drugs, and human trafficking. The regulatory application of these designated activities will be consistent with international standards, particularly the Financial Action Task Force (FATF) recommendations. This framework is designed to ensure that all financial institutions are subject to higher regulatory accountability.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP271

**Proposal name:** India Green Finance Facility (IGFF)

**Accredited entity:** Asian Development Bank

**Country:** The Republic of India

**Programme size:** Large

## L. Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview

The India Green Finance Facility (IGFF) proposal, submitted by the Asian Development Bank (ADB), aims to provide financing for energy efficiency and renewable energy projects in India. The facility will be funded by providing financing through a trust fund mechanism, leveraging USD 50 million in catalytic seed financing packages of USD 1 million each, with the potential to scale up to USD 271 million over time.

### 1.2 Programme structure and components

The IGFF is structured around two primary components, each with specific outputs and activities:

#### Component 1: Financing for development finance institutions (DFIs) to scale up emerging clean energy technologies (USD 50 million)

- This component provides financial support through the direct supply of funds.
- The component will provide technical assistance to DFIs for scaling up their portfolio and enhancing their capacity to manage risks associated with green financing.
- The financing will target key technology sectors such as rooftop solar photovoltaic (PV) systems, energy efficiency, and integrated renewable energy solutions including hybrid power plants.

#### Component 2: Technical assistance (USD 1.5 million)

- This component will provide technical assistance to DFIs for scaling up their portfolio and enhancing their capacity to manage risks associated with green financing.
- The financing will target key technology sectors such as rooftop solar PV systems, energy efficiency, and integrated renewable energy solutions including hybrid power plants.

# The IFF aims to address high-risk perceptions and stringent collateral requirements currently limiting financing for small-scale CDF projects.

## Component 1: Technical assistance (USD 300,000)

The technical assistance component will support financial institutions and green finance project proponents of the following:

### Outputs

#### 1.1 IFFI grant facilitates technical operations to enhance emerging finance arrangements (USD 4 million)

- This output will strengthen the financial capacity of at least five IFFI to prepare, appraise, and supervise green finance projects.
- Activities include developing appraisal tools, establishing monitoring systems, and providing training on green finance.

#### 1.2 NMFI and partnering financial institutions have enhanced technical operations to access credit guarantees through IFFI (USD 3 million)

- This output will support the development of structural facilities and enhance the IFFI effectively.
- Activities include developing appraisal tools, establishing monitoring systems, and providing training on project structuring and risk management.

#### 1.3 Public/private financial institutions adopt best practices to scale up green finance projects

- This output will report broader adoption of green finance practices among the financial sector.
- Activities include organizing financial sector dialogue, developing climate risk assessment tools, and aggregating best practices in scaling up green finance projects.

## Component 2: Technical support for mainstreaming green lending practices (USD 1.3 million)

The technical support component focuses on capacity building and technical assistance to enhance emerging finance arrangements.

### Outputs

#### 2.1 IFFI grant facilitates technical operations to enhance emerging finance arrangements (USD 4 million)

- This output will strengthen the financial capacity of at least five IFFI to prepare, appraise, and supervise green finance projects.
- Activities include developing appraisal tools, establishing monitoring systems, and providing training on green finance.

#### 2.2 NMFI and partnering financial institutions have enhanced technical operations to access credit guarantees through IFFI (USD 3 million)

- This output will support the development of structural facilities and enhance the IFFI effectively.
- Activities include developing appraisal tools, establishing monitoring systems, and providing training on project structuring and risk management.

#### 2.3 Public/private financial institutions adopt best practices to scale up green finance projects

- This output will report broader adoption of green finance practices among the financial sector.
- Activities include organizing financial sector dialogue, developing climate risk assessment tools, and aggregating best practices in scaling up green finance projects.

## 1.3 Financial instruments and terms

The IFF employs a blended finance approach with the following instruments:

### GO concessional loans (USD 87 million)

- USD 12.5 million for pre-blending IFFI units (Chapter 1).
- USD 60 million for on-lending to SMEs.
- Long-term include 20-year loan tenor, 3% per annum interest rate (including 0.5% per annum grace period), and 7-year grace period to IFFI loans, and 40-year maturity with a 10-year grace period for on-lending to SMEs.
- GO guarantees (USD 1.2 million).

# I. US million for capacity building related to the RSF (Output 2.1)

- US$ 2.85 million for broader financial sector capacity building (Output 2.3), and
- US$ 1.15 million for monitoring and evaluation.

## II. US million for technical assistance

- US$ 0.78 million in senior team, and
- US$ 0.50 million in technical assistance grants.

# 1.4 Expected impact and results

a. The RSF aims to deliver significant financial and development benefits:

i. Climate-resilient Targets: Target attainment of over 6.5 million metric tons of carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions reductions.
ii. Energy savings: Target savings for the region's power sector of renewable energy equivalent to the annual electricity consumption capacity for around 370,000 households or the annual energy use of nearly 800,000 battery energy storage units.
iii. Rural development: Target deployment of over 6,500 solar irrigation pumps and other rural electrification equipment.
iv. Sustainable transport: Target financing for more than 11,000 electric two- and three-wheelers.
v. Job creation: Target over 13,000 green jobs across the clean energy value chain, and
vi. Gender empowerment: Targeted support for women-led enterprises and gender-inclusive initiatives.

# 1.5 Implementation arrangements

a. The program will be implemented over a 10-year period, with an initial implementation of 27 months (July 2023 to September 2025), under the Bank's Active Financial Intermediation (AFI) framework for technical assistance and grants.

b. The RSF will approach addressing financing barriers for merging climate energy technologies in India, enhancing commercial finance with financial institutions, and leveraging the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to implement the climate grant and national development contributions (NDC) of India.

# II. Overall assessment

a. The RSF represents a strategic intervention to accelerate a clean energy transition for India by 2030 through targeted financing, technical assistance, and capacity building. The facility aligns with the country's nationally determined contribution (NDC) targets and the GCF funding requirements of US$ 1 billion in total investments and US$ 50 million in grants.

b. A key potential strength of the RSF is its integration with the broader GIF facility and related initiatives, which can enhance the reach and impact of the program. The program builds upon and complements existing efforts to promote clean energy transition in India.

# Green Climate Fund

## GCF's investment in India: including over USD 2.5 billion in sovereign energy sector lending over the next five years.

The GCF will leverage resources and ADB’s unique Project Preparation Facility (PPF) to support India’s transition towards a low-carbon economy by reducing greenhouse gas emissions, increasing renewable energy and improving the financial performance of distribution companies. The GCF will also support India’s efforts to increase its forest cover and enhance carbon sequestration through afforestation and reforestation.

### Key benefits:
- When the average cost of PPF assistance in terms of ending-conditionality payments is assumed to be 10% of the total project costs, the net benefit to India would be USD 2.4 billion over five years.
- The GCF will support India’s transition towards a low-carbon economy by reducing greenhouse gas emissions, increasing renewable energy and improving the financial performance of distribution companies. The GCF will also support India’s efforts to increase its forest cover and enhance carbon sequestration through afforestation and reforestation.

# power generation, transport and industry, which collectively account for the majority of emissions in India

## The role of the Green Technology Fund (GTF)

### 1.1
The GTF will provide support to the government's efforts to promote energy efficiency and renewable energy technologies. It will also help to address the challenges posed by the increasing demand for electricity, which is expected to grow at a compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of around 6% over the next decade. The GTF will focus on providing financial support to projects that aim to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and promote sustainable development.

### 1.2 Addressing needs of subnational governments and populations

#### 1.2.1
The GTF specifically targets subnational regions and vulnerable communities. The programme will help local states to cost effectively achieve their state-level climate change mitigation goals, while also promoting the adoption of clean energy technologies at the household level.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

#### 3.2.1 Potential to catalyse impact beyond the programme

The GTF designed to catalyse long-term systemic change in the clean energy financing landscape. By addressing the barriers to clean energy investment, the programme can help to unlock significant private sector finance and accelerate the transition to a low-carbon economy.

#### 3.2.2 Potential to address subnational governments' needs

The GTF will support state-level climate change mitigation efforts by providing financial assistance to states that are committed to reducing their greenhouse gas emissions. This will help to promote the adoption of clean energy technologies at the local level and contribute to the achievement of national climate goals.

#### 3.2.3 Potential to catalyse impact beyond the programme

The GTF designed to catalyse long-term systemic change in the clean energy financing landscape. By addressing the barriers to clean energy investment, the programme can help to unlock significant private sector finance and accelerate the transition to a low-carbon economy.

The IAF has also been working on developing a framework for the implementation of institutional arrangements. The approach involves a clear framework for scaling and replication through the development of a set of principles that can guide the scaling-up process. The IAF GCF projects have been designed to be scalable and replicable, with a focus on building capacity and promoting innovation.

# Contributions to regulatory frameworks and policies

## a)
The GEF contributes significantly to improving the regulatory and policy environment for climate finance in India. Component 4 of the programme includes technical assistance for developing regulatory frameworks that support the integration of renewable energy into the grid, with both Renewable Energy Funds from the government and international financial institutions. This will help to improve the regulatory framework and policies related to climate change.

## b)
The programme will support the development of standardized green lending criteria. It will also provide technical assistance for developing green credit lines and other financing instruments that strengthen the overall enabling environment.

## c)
In response to GEF priority 420 on alignment with emerging green finance mechanisms, the programme will support the implementation of green financial standards and policies. This includes promoting regulatory frameworks being developed by the government to support climate change mitigation and adaptation, including specific activities to help DFIs adopt these frameworks.

# Sustainable development potential

## Scale: Low-Medium

### i)
The GEF provides a comprehensive approach to its sustainable development benefits.

### ii)
Environmental benefits include improved air quality from reduced fuel consumption, increased energy efficiency, and better waste management. These CER projects also contribute to the conservation of biodiversity and ecosystems by reducing greenhouse gas emissions.

### iii)
Social benefits include improved livelihoods for local communities through employment opportunities in project implementation and operation. The programme will also support the development of green jobs and skills training for local workers.

### iv)
Economic benefits include increased investment in renewable energy projects, improved health outcomes from reduced air pollution, and increased energy security. These activities will also contribute to the development of a green economy by promoting sustainable practices and technologies.

# Environmental and social risk management

## The GEF provides a comprehensive approach to environmental and social risk management (ESRM) to identify, assess and manage environmental and social risks associated with programme components.


---

# Green Climate Fund

## Section Title

### Subsection Title

Activities under the Green Climate Fund’s investment plan that are controlled environmental and social management frameworks (ESMF) can develop a risk program's objectives with ESF's support to ensure that risks are identified, assessed, and managed effectively. The ESMF is designed to be flexible enough to accommodate different types of projects and programs.

The ESMF includes screening criteria for projects, risk management procedures, and monitoring indicators. It also provides guidance on how to develop an ESMF tailored to the specific needs of a project or program. The ESMF is designed to be used in conjunction with other social risk tools, such as high-risk (Category A) projects excluded from programmed financing.

When the ESMF does not address a particular type of risk, it can be supplemented by additional guidance and procedures tailored to that specific risk. The ESMF provides detailed guidance on how to develop an ESMF for different types of projects and programs. It also provides guidance on how to integrate the ESMF into existing project management systems.

However, critical consensus exists between the risk identification and the actual implementation of the ESMF. There is a need to ensure that the risk assessment process is consistent with the risk management framework. This requires a comprehensive understanding of the risks associated with different types of projects and programs. More concerning is the lack of a requirement to consider the potential impacts on local communities, including indigenous peoples.

In addition, there are concerns about the lack of a clear process for addressing country-specific patterns in climate change protocols where technical approaches identify critical areas that have been identified as high risk but are not included in the ESMF. This raises questions about whether the risks associated with these areas should be addressed through the ESMF or through other mechanisms. The latter may be more appropriate, given the need to address specific local contexts and the potential for conflict between different types of projects.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: Medium-High

##### 3.4.1 Addressing country and group vulnerability

The Green Climate Fund’s investment plan aims to achieve long-term impacts and energy security objectives. The financial assessment framework validates the recipient's ability to manage risks effectively, with a focus on reducing vulnerability to climate change. This includes addressing the needs of vulnerable groups and ensuring that projects are designed to be resilient to climate change.

The program targets technologies that enhance climate resilience in vulnerable sectors such as agriculture, water, and energy. It also supports organizational development for climate change adaptation and mitigation. The program aims to improve the resilience of communities and ecosystems by investing in sustainable infrastructure and promoting climate-smart practices.

By focusing on underdeveloped regions, including the north and east, the Green Climate Fund can help address the needs of vulnerable groups and promote sustainable development.

# IV. Key Issues and Recommendations

## 4.1 Risk-sharing facility structure and management

### a.
The Secretary has long assessed that "No Accident Early" is not achievable also to complete the risk discharge on the proposed structure of the risk sharing before water is discharged from the dam. The Secretary recommends that the RSP should be designed to reflect the risks associated with the operation of the development facility (DFP) conducted by the proponent and the risks associated with the operation of the development facility.

### b.
In response to TAF appointment #2 on DFP management, ABS concluded that the RSP will not be able to manage the risk associated with the operation of the development facility. The Secretary recommends that the RSP should be designed to reflect the risks associated with the operation of the development facility and the risks associated with the operation of the development facility.

### c.
To address uncertainty around the risk discharge, ABS should finalize the RSP design to reflect the risks associated with the operation of the development facility. The Secretary recommends that the RSP should be designed to reflect the risks associated with the operation of the development facility and the risks associated with the operation of the development facility.

## 4.2 Technology selection and financial viability

### a.
The Secretary notes concerns about the financial viability of some technologies, particularly those that are not yet commercially proven. The Secretary recommends that the WACC applied during cost (capital) and the project's feasibility depends on the technology selected for the project.

### b.
In response to TAF appointment #4, ABS acknowledged these challenges but explained that the risks associated with the operation of the development facility are not yet fully understood. While this is a valid concern, the Secretary recommends that the RSP should be designed to reflect the risks associated with the operation of the development facility and the risks associated with the operation of the development facility.

### c.
While the DFP technology selection process is a long-term technology assessment aligned with the energy transition priorities of both the hydrogen component of the MIP, ABS acknowledges that the risks associated with the operation of the development facility are not yet fully understood. While this is a valid concern, the Secretary recommends that the RSP should be designed to reflect the risks associated with the operation of the development facility and the risks associated with the operation of the development facility.

# Further reference during early implementation

GCF may wish to request that ARR provide updates on progress in addressing these recommendations within the first year of implementation.

## The CAP recommends that the all countries the following measures:

### Strengthen institutional climate management

- **ix.** Countries should ensure that DFIs are involved and possibly include comprehensive climate strategies as part of their engagement with GCF accredited financing institutions. This would help to ensure that DFIs are aware of the importance of climate change in their operations, and can provide support for deep institutional change. The programme could benefit from ensuring DFIs are included in the programme’s activities and events. This would also help to ensure that DFIs are able to share best practice and lessons learned with other DFIs. It is important to note that this measure would apply only where GCF resources are engaged, meaning that DFIs would be expected to implement these measures using their own resources.

### Enhance technology-specific implementation strategies

- **ix.** Give the varying maturity levels and risk profiles of the five target technologies, the programme should consider providing targeted support for each. This could include: (a) supporting countries with low maturity in a particular technology through capacity building; (b) supporting countries with high maturity to develop roadmaps with their development partners for the hydrogen economy; (c) supporting countries with high maturity to develop roadmaps with their development partners for the hydrogen economy; and (d) supporting countries with high maturity to develop roadmaps with their development partners for the hydrogen economy. This could include: (a) supporting countries with low maturity in a particular technology through capacity building; (b) supporting countries with high maturity to develop roadmaps with their development partners for the hydrogen economy.

### What regarding strategies, other than working through DFIs?

- **ix.** The programme could consider providing targeted support to countries that have not engaged with DFIs. This would include: (a) identifying countries that have not engaged with DFIs; (b) conducting a needs assessment of these countries and their potential demand for the hydrogen economy; and (c) supporting these countries in developing roadmaps with potential development partners.

### Strengthen monitoring and adaptive management

- **ix.** The programme would benefit from establishing an enhanced Monitoring Framework that includes indicators to track progress towards achieving its objectives. This could include: (a) developing a regular survey of countries and market conditions; (b) conducting periodic evaluations of technology readiness and adoption rates; and (c) supporting the development of a robust monitoring system that can be used to inform adaptive management decisions. The programme should also consider establishing a process for validating these indicators against operational frameworks.

### The CAP recommends that the trust approve this funding proposal.
