# 1. BACKGROUND

1. The Andhra Pradesh Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAAP) for the India Green Finance Facility (hereafter referred to as "GFF" or "Facility"), known as GAT, outlines the gender-related challenges and opportunities in green finance. It aims to achieve gender equality by addressing the gaps in the existing policies of both the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the International Finance Corporation's (IFC) gender policy. The Facility seeks to enhance the role of women (as consumers, employees, and entrepreneurs) in each of the following areas: access to finance, employment opportunities, and the implementation of low-carbon energy solutions, including:
   - Round-the-clock (RTC) renewable energy with storage to ensure 24/7 clean power supply.
   - Decentralized renewable energy for rural applications, improving livelihoods and resilience.
   - Electric mobility solutions for rural and peri-urban transport to decarbonize mobility.
   - Gender-responsive climate-smart agriculture.

2. The Facility also includes the Gender Action Plan (GAP), which outlines specific actions that the IFC will take to ensure gender equality in its work with the GCF. These include:
   - Integrating gender considerations into all IFC operations and projects for the life of the Facility.
   - Ensuring that at least 50% of the IFC's climate-related investments are directed towards women-owned or women-led businesses.
   - Increasing the number of women in leadership positions within the IFC.

2. INTRODUCTION TO THE INDIA GREEN FINANCE FACILITY

## 1. Background and Rationale

The India Green Finance Facility (GFF) is a structured finance facility designed to accelerate India's energy transition through targeted financing to scale up renewable energy, energy efficiency, and climate resilience investments. The Facility will be implemented by the IFC in collaboration with the GCF, which will provide concessional funding to support the GFF. The GFF aims to achieve the following objectives:
   - Enhancing access to affordable finance for renewable energy projects.
   - Promoting the adoption of clean technologies and climate-smart solutions.
   - Supporting the development of technical assistance to strengthen project pipelines, address market barriers, and foster innovation in green finance.
   - Encouraging the deployment of gender-responsive financing mechanisms that advance women's economic empowerment and value chain integration.

The Facility includes:
   - Powering India Energy Transition including RTC renewable energy with storage and decentralized renewable energy solutions.

# Modernizing Energy Access for Cleaner Living

## Including Electric mobility solutions for rural and semi-urban transport, and CBDT's sustainable water management initiatives

### 1. Clean energy access

- **Energy efficiency**: Promote efficient use of electricity.
- **Decentralized renewable energy (DRE)**: Promote solar water pumps and cold storage.

### 2. Integrated Green Energy Solutions

- **IGFF strengthened**: DFI capabilities to integrate practices into their operations that adopt clean energy solutions.
- **Green financing**: Encourage lending for green products, and technical assistance programs.
    - **Effects**: Enables sustainable development, reduces carbon footprint, and promotes cleaner air.
    - **Benefits**: Cleaner energy solutions reduce greenhouse gas emissions, leading to better health outcomes.

### 3. Gender equality

- **Gender-responsive financing**: Ensure gender-responsiveness in financing.
- **IGFF strengthened**: DFI capabilities to integrate practices into their operations that adopt clean energy solutions and promote green products.
    - **Outcomes**: Gender-responsive and aligned with the GGF Gender Policy and Action Plan.

### 4. The Facility is structured into two components

- **Component 1**: Financing for DFIs to scale up lending for clean energy technologies.
- **Component 2**: Technical support for DFIs to build capacity in green finance, including green credit lines and green loan products.
    - **Outcome**: Enhanced capacity in DFI's to promote green lending practices.

### 5. By providing this integrated package of TA and low-cost financing, the Facility supports

- **DFI's** to scale up their clean energy portfolios, reducing carbon intensity and receiving funding from (GGF) resources.
- **Women**: Promote women's access to clean energy solutions.

### 6. When the IGFF is granted based on sound goals, it also promotes sustainable development and inclusive growth

- **DFI's** are encouraged to adopt green lending practices.
    - **Outcome**: Enhanced capacity in DFI's to promote green lending practices.
    - **Benefits**: Promotes sustainable development, reduces carbon intensity, and enhances women's access to clean energy solutions.

### 7. These benefits can be realized through enhanced partnerships of women in the energy sector

- **Women**: Promote women's access to clean energy solutions.

---

# APPROACH

1. The success of gender mainstreaming in the GFF hinges on the active involvement of the DFIs, and depends to a large extent on their leadership or reorientation towards gender equality. This approach will be implemented through the following strategies:
   - Enhancing the capacity of DFIs to integrate gender into their policies, procedures, and operations.
   - Promoting the development and implementation of concrete projects, and 1) identifying women’s needs and opportunities; 2) ensuring that women are beneficiaries of these projects; and 3) promoting women’s leadership in the energy sector.

2. The Technical Assistance (TA) provided to a DFI under component 2 can be used to support gender mainstreaming efforts. This includes:
   - Developing TA to support DFIs’ mainstreaming, monitoring, and evaluation including modeling and forecasting for gendered activities in the clean energy sector.
     - Capacity building on gender-responsive approaches and methodologies.
     - Data collection frameworks, including both to monitor financing, funds, implementation, and impact of gender-related projects.
   - Establishing preferential financing terms. Collaborate with DFIs and other stakeholders to develop and implement preferential financing terms that promote the empowerment of women in the clean energy value chain.
   - Support to women-led enterprises. Provide support to women in start-ups, SMEs, and entrepreneurship training programs, aimed at equipping women with the skills and abilities needed to advance their businesses and create jobs.

3. Support dialogue to advocate for policy frameworks that address gender equality issues:
   - Strengthen engagement in policy advocacy. Facilitate 1) structural dialogue between government agencies, DFIs, and private sector; and 2) promote the development of policies that support gender-inclusive clean energy financing.
   - Promote regulatory alignment. Work alongside regulators such as central banks to ensure that regulations are aligned with the principles of gender equality in green finance.

# S.1 Gender Frameworks, Policies and Guidelines

## 12
Managed by ADB, this Facility will provide co-financing from GCF-exploited synergies to support gender mainstreaming in ADB operations. It aims to (a) enhance the capacity of the Facility to assist in executing ADB projects, guidelines and instruments on gender mainstreaming; (b) GCF cofinance new and (c) the existing gender-related projects and programs.

## 13
When gender mainstreaming with the GF is a subset, the Facility seeks to (a) enhance the capacity of the Facility to assist in executing ADB projects, guidelines and instruments on gender mainstreaming; (b) GCF cofinance new and (c) the existing gender-related projects and programs. When gender is the primary objective, the Facility provides the necessary support to strengthen institutional capacity for gender equality and social inclusion through (a) knowledge management and (b) research and development of gender-responsive policies and procedures. The Facility will also provide technical assistance to support targeted operations to empower women and girls and promote gender mainstreaming that supports the Sustainable Development Goals.

## 14
One of the key operational principles of ADB's Strategy 2030 is gender equality in Asia and the Pacific. Gender equality is a prerequisite for inclusive economic growth, poverty reduction, and resilience to external shocks. The ADB Gender Equality Policy Framework provides the basis for the implementation of the four optional priority policy pillars of Strategy 2030: (a) gender equality in operations; (b) gender equality in the workplace; (c) support targeted operations to empower women and girls; and (d) promote gender mainstreaming that supports the Sustainable Development Goals.

## 15
ADB's Gender Equality Framework (GEF) and Corporate Results Framework (CRF) provide the basis for reporting on ADB operational and organizational results. The GEF is a framework that aligns with ADB's strategic objectives and operational priorities, and it provides the basis for reporting on ADB operational and organizational results.

## 16
Under the ADB gender mainstreaming framework, a project is designed as one of the following:

### a. Gender Equality Theme (GET)
GET where the project explicitly includes gender equality as an outcome or empowerment. A project is assigned GET if the project's primary objective is to achieve gender equality and/or empower women and girls through targeted services, center economic.

# Effective Gender Mainstreaming (EGM)
A project is designed EGM to the project outcome to lead gender equality or women's empowerment, but project outputs are not necessarily gender-specific.

## Gender-Gender Equality (GGE)
A project is assigned GGE if it aims to directly improve women's access to social services. Technical infrastructure and economic resources are provided to women to enhance their status in society.
### Integrated Gender Equality (IGE)
A project is assigned IGE if it is not expected to directly benefit a woman, though there may be indirect benefits for women.

## Gender Mainstreaming
Gender mainstreaming aims to integrate gender perspectives into the policies and programs of various sectors. This includes:
- Ensuring equal participation in decision-making processes.
- Promoting gender equality in all aspects of life.
- Encouraging women's involvement in sustainable development, with a focus on reducing inequalities.

## Gender Mainstreaming Goals
Gender mainstreaming goals include:
- Increasing women's access to education and training.
- Improving the status of women in the labor force.
- Promoting gender equality in all aspects of life.

# 3.2. GFF Methodology

## 20.

GFF will enhance climate-related projects or interventions (through GFs) that involve sectors, response outcomes to low GHG emissions and climate-related development benefits. GFF will also support projects that address the needs of vulnerable groups and sectors, including gender and social inclusion criteria, climate resilience, and project areas that benefit applicable to women.

## 21.

All projects must meet specific gender or social inclusion criteria outlined in the Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP) and Gender Equality Policy (GEP). These criteria will be tailored to each project's context and aligned with broader processes arranged in collaboration with GFs.

# 3.2.1 Gender Requirements of GFF and Operation (genderprogramme)

## 22.

Gender equality is the core value of the GFF. The gender mainstreaming framework and policy will guide the implementation of all projects. GFF will ensure that gender equality is integrated into all aspects of project design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation through selected GFs where this is a GC-allocated national only. GFF will review the gender equality action plan (GEAP) and gender equality policy (GEP). The GEAP and GEP will be reviewed annually to ensure that they are relevant, comprehensive, and aligned with the overall objectives of the GFF. The GEAP and GEP will also be reviewed by the Gender Equality Committee (GEC) to address and support the implementation of the GEAP and GEP.

## 23.

Gender and social inclusion criteria that guide the selection of people projects at the local level should be aligned with the context of gender interventions relevant to the country. The criteria should be based on the needs of the community, including the most vulnerable groups.

# Gender Categorization and GAPs

## 24.

All projects will advance gender considerations to promote inclusive participation in climate-related projects. Gender Equality Action Plans (GEAP) and Gender Equality Policies (GEP) will prepare a detailed GEAP for each project. The GEAP will be aligned with the overall objectives of the GFF and the specific needs of the community.

## 25.

All areas will be categorized as Effective Gender Mainstreaming (EGM) according to ADB's Gender and Development Framework Each project

measurable and time-bound targets, and its alignment with IGF’s overarching gender goals. The PMU is aware and updates at GPs’ and gender categorization during the design phase.

### Appraisal Process

25. The GPs and gender categorization will undergo validation at both the GP and IGF levels and the PMU to ensure that GPs align with IGF strategic objectives before they are implemented.

### Implementation and Monitoring

26. Approved projects must adhere to their GPs and submit regular reports, including progress updates on gender-related activities. The PMU will monitor these reports and provide feedback based on reporting findings on gender KPIs and GP implementation to the IGF. These reports will be reviewed by the PMU and shared with AOB members during quarterly meetings.

27. It is important to note that not all IGF projects will contribute to all the gender equality goals. However, it is essential to ensure that each project contributes to at least one thematic sector. The identification of which gender objectives that are most relevant and achievable should be based on the context and resources available for GP implementation. AOB principal members will be consulted during the GP appraisal process to ensure alignment with existing implementation challenges.

### Capacity Building and Technical Assistance

28. To ensure that GPs have the necessary capacity to support gender work at the project level, the PMU will provide technical assistance and training. This includes the implementation of specific GPs, Gender Action Plans, as well as the integration of gender into the IGF’s overall work. The PMU will also provide technical assistance in achieving gender equality objectives.

### New and Existing Program Management Unit (PMU) at the Facility Level

29.3.2 A new or existing Program Management Unit (PMU) at the facility level is essential for implementing, ensuring strategic alignment, technical support, and guidance across all GPs. At a general minimum, the PMU will establish a Gender Implementation Unit (GIU), also known as a gender focal point, to manage IGF projects, operations, and activities related to gender equality. This unit will be responsible for knowledge sharing and capacity building, the facility-level team will integrate regular gender-related objectives into their work plans.

# DFPs: DFPs will be formally responsible for the preparation of projects and programs Gender Assessments and Action Plans. They are also responsible for monitoring project implementation and reporting on progress to the DFF. The gender assessment will be a key component of the project implementation. Should a new DFI gain the DFF, due diligence assessment will be required.

## ADB as the Accountable Entity

ADB as the Accountable Entity will be responsible for implementing and ensuring a government mechanism to enhance the Family as PBL. ADB will regularly report on progress to the DFF. The Family as PBL will be subject to regular review by the DFF, and other relevant stakeholders, which will be promoted by the DFF. The Family as PBL will be a key component of the gender assessment process. ADB will be the institutional and technical gender consultant mentioned above, in order to ensure that the Family as PBL is implemented effectively.

## The PML

The PML, guided by OGB, will provide IAB (a fully integrated project management department) of DFPs, ADB PML will be responsible for the initial Due Diligence of all DFI gender assessments and action plans. The PML will also be responsible for ensuring that the gender assessments are Reporting sex-disaggregated data to GCF (green and social level).

# GENDER ASSESSMENT

The section provides a comprehensive analysis on the national laws and policy framework governing the rights of women and girls in India. It also includes an overview of the existing gender policies, objectives and strategies that have been implemented by the Government. The Family as PBL aims to strengthen the understanding between men and women in India. It will be a key component of the gender assessment process. ## 4.1 National Laws and Policy Framework of Gender

The government of India has demonstrated its commitment to building an enabling environment for gender equality through the adoption of key international agreements and conventions. The Constitution of India prohibits discrimination on the basis of sex, caste, religion, race, and place of birth. The Indian government has also ratified the Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The government has also adopted the National Policy for the Empowerment of Women in 2001. The government has also established a comprehensive policy framework identifying 12 critical areas for achieving gender equality and women's empowerment. The government has also ratified several international treaties and conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Convention on the Rights of the Child, and the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities.


---
**Page 143**
---

# Rights of Persons with Disabilities, among others

These further guide the gender-inclusive commitments of India towards the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development.

## Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016

The Act provides for equal opportunities and participation of persons with disabilities. The Act was passed in 1995. The National Commission for Persons with Disabilities was founded to safeguard the rights of persons with disabilities. The Act was amended in 2016 to include seven categories of disabilities, including mental illness, specific learning disabilities, and neurological conditions. It also includes an equal opportunity binding obligation, which is only the third Gender Budget Statement (GBS) of India to include a gender budget statement. The GBS is published annually to provide an overview of public expenditures in various ministries through a gender lens.

### In India of Modernity

India has undertaken significant legislative measures to promote gender equality and women's empowerment. The Maternity Benefit Act amended in 2017 provides for 26 weeks of maternity leave, up from 12 weeks earlier. The Act also includes provisions for adoptive mothers and commissioning mothers. Further, the passage of the Women's Reservation Bill in 2010 at the national level is a significant step towards women's empowerment. The bill aims to reserve 33% of seats in the Lok Sabha and state legislative assemblies for women.

### The CEDAW Committee's Concluding Observations on the Eighth and Ninth Periodic Reports

The CEDAW Committee has expressed concern about the lack of implementation of its previous recommendations. The Committee recommended that India take steps to ensure gender equality in all areas of life, including education, health, and employment. The Committee also noted the lack of progress in implementing the recommendations of the 2013 report and the need for a comprehensive approach to women's empowerment.

### UN Women

- **2023**: Highlights the importance of gender-responsive budgeting in achieving gender equality.

- **2020**: The Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) reviewed India's progress.
- **2019**: The Committee on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities reviewed India's progress.
- **2018**: The Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights reviewed India's progress.

# India's Progress on Gender Equality

India has made significant progress in building an enabling environment for gender equality through comprehensive legal and policy frameworks. However, gaps and challenges remain in achieving substantive equality.

The National Human Rights Commission (NHRC) has highlighted that the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic have exacerbated existing inequalities and deepened discrimination against women and marginalized communities. The NHRC has noted the increased vulnerability of women to gender-based violence during the pandemic and the need for targeted interventions to address these issues.

On violence against women, the CEDAW Committee also noted the efforts of the Indian government in addressing various forms of violence against women. However, the Committee is still concerned about the value of increasing reporting on domestic violence cases and the effectiveness of support services provided to survivors. The National Crime Records Bureau (NCRB) reported 4,05,861 cases of crime against women in 2021, including 37,924 cases of rape.

The NHRC has also highlighted the impact of traditional gender roles and societal expectations on women's employment and economic empowerment. Women are often limited to low-paying jobs due to their responsibilities at home and face discrimination in the workplace. The NHRC has noted that women are more likely to be employed in the informal sector, which lacks job security and social protection.

The NHRC has also highlighted the impact of traditional gender roles and societal expectations on women's employment and economic empowerment. Women are often limited to low-paying jobs due to their responsibilities at home and face discrimination in the workplace. The NHRC has noted that women are more likely to be employed in the informal sector, which lacks job security and social protection.

Women are often limited to low-paying jobs due to their responsibilities at home and face discrimination in the workplace. # 4.3 Gendered Disparities in Energy Access in India: Challenges and Opportunities

## 4.3.1 Gendered Disparities in Energy Access

Energy access in rural communities is a significant gender disparity, particularly between men and women and among marginalized populations. Household energy use is often determined by the time available for collecting fuelwood and other traditional fuels (IEA, 2021). This task disproportionately falls on women, who spend an average of 25-48 hours per week collecting fuelwood and other traditional fuels.

Women have better access to modern energy sources than men. For instance, only 37% of households using LPG as their primary cooking fuel have constant supply for less than one hour a day. Women are more likely to use traditional biomass-based cooking systems such as chulhas and biomass stoves, which are less efficient and cause indoor air pollution.

Women also face challenges in accessing energy services through utilities such as the Backdrop Scheme. Approximately 65% of rural households do not have access to electricity, and women are disproportionately affected by this lack of access. Women's health is negatively impacted by exposure to indoor air pollution, which contributes to respiratory diseases, cancer, and other health issues.

Women also face challenges in accessing energy services through utilities such as the Backdrop Scheme. Approximately 65% of rural households do not have access to electricity, and women are disproportionately affected by this lack of access. Women's health is negatively impacted by exposure to indoor air pollution, which contributes to respiratory diseases, cancer, and other health issues.

Women also face challenges in accessing energy services through utilities such as the Backdrop Scheme. Approximately 65% of rural households do not have access to electricity, and women are disproportionately affected by this lack of access. Women's health is negatively impacted by exposure to indoor air pollution, which contributes to respiratory diseases, cancer, and other health issues.

# About 14.5% in India are female-headed households; among such single mothers are the most economically disadvantaged with 42%, belonging to the lowest social class. They have low levels of education and skills, and also lack health to earn a living in respect to male household heads (80% vs. 76%). The main reasons for their vulnerability include lower income, higher poverty rates, lower savings, lower asset ownership, safety concerns, lack of professional networks, and social isolation.

The grant for the creation of a gig with disabilities and minorities was also expressed in CEGAW. ## Gender Inequality

Gender inequalities are strongly associated with social and regional disparities in India. Women's participation in the labor force is low, with female labor force participation rates as high as 35% in some regions and as low as 10%. The gender gap in labor force participation is higher among women from lower-income households, rural areas, and lower levels of education. In addition, there are significant regional differences in the level of gender inequality.

## Opportunities for Progressive Change

The study shows that empowering women through targeted interventions can improve their economic status and reduce poverty. The use of microfinance systems, such as self-help groups and business loans, has been shown to be effective in improving the lives of women and children. In addition, the provision of education and training opportunities for women can help them gain the skills they need to succeed in the labor market.

### 52. AEI’s international programs align with these initiatives, emphasizing gender equality and women’s empowerment through energy access. Programs include:

- **Subsidized financing** for women entrepreneurs in renewable energy and clean cooking sectors.
- **Capacity building** to enhance women’s skills and confidence in managing energy assets.
- **Advocacy efforts** such as the Panchayat Mahila Upaya Yojana (PMUY), which promotes women’s participation in local governance and can further improve outcomes[^2].

### 53. Research on the approaches of agencies in addressing gender disparities in energy access is crucial for enhancing gender equality. This includes:

- **Identifying social, cultural, and economic barriers** to women’s participation in energy interventions.
- **Promoting gender-responsive policies** that ensure equal access to energy services.
- **Encouraging community-based initiatives** that empower women through energy access.
- **Advocating for the integration of gender-disaggregated data collection and research** to challenge stereotypes and biases, ensuring that women’s needs are met in renewable energy programs.

### 4.4. Women in the renewable energy workforce in India

#### 56. Women are leaders in many small-scale enterprises across India, particularly in rural areas. Cultural and traditional norms often restrict women’s employment opportunities, particularly in industries heavily dominated by men. However, women’s participation in the sector is further fueled by their involvement as:

- **Entrepreneurs** who start businesses in renewable energy.
- **Technicians** who install and maintain solar systems.
- **Sales agents** who market and sell renewable energy products.

Additionally, formal education and training in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) fields are increasingly important for women’s participation. Addressing these challenges requires comprehensive policies that support women’s empowerment, including:

- **Skills development programs** to enhance women’s technical skills.
- **Incentives** to encourage women’s participation in the renewable energy workforce.

# India's female energy sector participation

India has over five million women working in the energy sector, with India being a market and not just a labor via consideration while assessing programs and projects. The low number of women in STEM education is due to cultural norms and religious beliefs that restrict their mobility and access to education. However, the government has taken steps to increase female participation in the energy sector by providing scholarships and training programs.

## 58. India's female energy sector participation

India's female energy sector participation is expanding rapidly, creating significant opportunities for women's economic empowerment. However, women's participation in the energy sector remains low due to cultural norms and religious beliefs that restrict their mobility and access to education. The government has taken steps to increase female participation in the energy sector by providing scholarships and training programs.

## 59. Transition from education to workforce

Despite high STEM graduation rates (only about 14% of STEM-qualified women pursue careers as scientists, engineers, or technicians), women are significantly underrepresented at higher organizational levels, with women making up only 20-30% of senior management positions.

## 60. Women's participation in the renewable energy sector

Women constitute less than 15% of India's solar energy workforce, compared to the global average of 32%. The lack of female representation in the renewable energy sector is due to cultural norms and religious beliefs that restrict their mobility and access to education.

## 61. Barriers to participation

Women face barriers to industry-aligned education and vocational training programs in renewable energy, with the perception of technical jobs being physically demanding and dangerous. The lack of female role models in the renewable energy sector encourages unequal gender roles and stereotypes, which are compounded by cultural and social norms.

## 62. Recommendations for increasing women's participation

Women need better access to industry-aligned education and vocational training programs in renewable energy, with a particular focus on technical skills development. The government should also promote the benefits of renewable energy careers to encourage more women to enter the field. Additionally, cultural and social norms often restrict women's mobility and access to education.

# Discouraging women from pursuing careers in traditionally male-dominated fields like energy, further limiting their opportunities

## Addressing barriers and promoting inclusion
The International Energy Agency (IEA) has launched a new program to enhance women's participation in the workforce. The IEA of New and Renewable Energy (INRE) often referred to as INRE, is an international organization that promotes policies aimed at enhancing energy efficiency and sustainable energy use. Similarly, the Council for Women in Energy and Environmental Engineering (CWEEL) and the Council on Energy, Environment, and Water (CEEW) are also working towards women's inclusion in energy innovation, maintenance, and energy entrepreneurship.

## Private sector companies are also taking steps to improve gender diversity
Companies such as BP, Shell, and ExxonMobil have implemented policies aimed at increasing the number of women in their workforce. These policies include flexible work arrangements, mentoring programs, and targeted salary measures such as PPF (Performance-based Pay) designed for women's family needs. Additionally, these companies are working to create a more inclusive environment by addressing unconscious bias and providing training on gender balance. This helps women achieve their professional goals.

### 4.5 Gendered dynamics across the GFF focus areas
#### 65. In the energy sector we look at gender dynamics across the three broad objectives of GFF outcomes in the Founding Protocol

#### 6.2.1 Women and powering India's energy transition
Women are actively involved in various aspects of India's energy transition projects, including planning, project management, technical implementation, policy development, and advocacy programs.

## Barriers to women's participation in the energy sector
Women face significant barriers in the energy sector, including lack of access to education, training, and mentoring. Additionally, they often encounter gender bias and discrimination in the workplace. Despite these challenges, women are making significant contributions to the energy sector, particularly in areas such as renewable energy and energy efficiency.

## Women working on projects in this sector need a range of skills to contribute effectively
Women working in the energy sector require a diverse set of skills, including technical expertise, project management, data analysis, and policy development. These skills are essential for addressing climate change, improving energy access, and promoting sustainable development. Women's participation in these areas is crucial for achieving gender equality and ensuring that the benefits of energy transition are shared equitably.

## Gender diversity in energy

There is currently a low level of girls among women in India's large-scale energy sector. The Women in Renewable Energy (WIRE) initiative aims at the transformation of gender biases in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) education. Women account for less than 30% of the workforce in renewable energy projects. The WIRE initiative aims to enhance women's participation in renewable energy projects by providing mentorship, training, and networking opportunities. The Skill Council for Green Jobs (SCGJ), after vocational training programs, specifically designed for women, has launched a campaign to promote gender diversity in the sector.

### Women in Renewable Energy (WIRE)

The WIRE initiative is a joint effort between the National Skills Network (NSN) and the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA). The NSN is a platform that aims to facilitate hands-on experience and exposure for women in renewable energy projects. The IRENA is an intergovernmental organization that promotes the use of renewable energy sources.

### 4.5.2.1 Energy needs and household fuel choices

Women’s energy needs are influenced by their domestic responsibilities for cooking, heating, lighting, water supply, and other household activities. Choices for fuel use depend on availability, affordability, and preferences regarding convenience, safety, and environmental impact. Women’s preferences can be influenced by cultural norms, health concerns, and the need to balance multiple roles within the family. Women can be employed in various roles within IFC solar projects, including technical sales, installation, and maintenance. Women’s involvement in community engagement activities can help address local concerns about energy access and gender issues. Women’s empowerment can also enhance their ability to advocate for clean cooking solutions and influence household decisions on energy use. Women’s empowerment can also enhance their ability to advocate for clean cooking solutions and influence household decisions on energy use.

### 4.5.2.1.1 Household fuel choices

Households choose fuels based on availability, cost, convenience, and cultural preferences. Women play a key role in selecting fuels and managing fuel supplies within the household. Women’s preferences can be influenced by their domestic responsibilities, including cooking, heating, lighting, water supply, and other household activities. Women’s energy needs are influenced by their domestic responsibilities for cooking, heating, lighting, water supply, and other household activities.

### 4.5.2.1.2 Clean energy choices

Clean energy choices have a direct correlation with the improved health of women. For example, switching from biomass to clean stoves reduces indoor air pollution, which is linked to respiratory diseases such as asthma, bronchitis, and pneumonia in children. This shift also boosts productivity by freeing up time previously spent on fuel collection. Clean cooking solutions can significantly improve women’s access to clean energy sources, reducing their exposure to harmful pollutants. Clean cooking solutions can significantly improve women’s access to clean energy sources, reducing their exposure to harmful pollutants.

For example, switching from biomass to clean stoves reduces indoor air pollution, which is linked to respiratory diseases such as asthma, bronchitis, and pneumonia in children. This shift also boosts productivity by freeing up time previously spent on fuel collection. Clean cooking solutions can significantly improve women’s access to clean energy sources, reducing their exposure to harmful pollutants.

### 4.5.2.1.7 Women’s empowerment

Women’s empowerment is closely linked to women’s access to clean energy choices and the overall health of the family. Women’s empowerment can also enhance their ability to advocate for clean cooking solutions and influence household decisions on energy use.


---
**Page 154**
---

Women’s involvement in agriculture, which provides foodstuffs for human consumption, such as crop residues, animal manure, and organic waste, is crucial for renewable energy production. Their participation is essential to ensure that women are not only beneficiaries but also active participants in the clean energy transition.

### 77.
Despite these opportunities, there are significant barriers. Gender norms often limit women’s access to resources, particularly in STEM fields, which are critical for advancing in the renewable energy sector. Women face numerous challenges, including limited access to education and training, lack of financial support that enables them to only make up 32% of the global renewable energy workforce, and in many cases, they are excluded from decision-making processes related to safety concerns and gender equity issues. Also, women’s participation in energy-related activities is often overlooked, resulting in a lack of recognition for their contributions. Women’s involvement in the clean energy transition is crucial as it can lead to improved access to renewable energy resources.

### 18.
The energy industry presents a unique opportunity for women to play a leading role in achieving a more sustainable future. The sector is male-dominated and often multiple entry points for women’s participation exist, including operations, maintenance, research and development, and project management. As noted above, a significant proportion of the renewable energy workforce is female (32%), higher than in traditional energy sectors, but this does not translate into equal opportunities or representation at all levels. Women are still underrepresented in leadership positions, and they often face barriers to career advancement due to gender bias, lack of mentorship, and limited access to training and professional development opportunities. This creates a more diverse and equitable renewable energy workforce.

# In transportation, empowering women through green mobility systems can be equally transformative.

Safe, accessible, and gender-sensitive transport systems also serve and benefit men and boys, as well as individuals with disabilities, the elderly, and other marginalized groups who have specific needs. According to UN Women, a lack of safe and reliable transport is one of the biggest barriers to women’s economic empowerment and participation in the labor force.

The Gender Equality (GE) Action Plan, with its targets and as part of the sustainable development goals (SDGs), can accelerate best practices for gender-responsive planning. The GE Action Plan pays a due role in identifying green mobility solutions that help meet net-zero emissions by 2050. It includes “Mobility for all,” which aims to ensure that women have equal access to safe, affordable, and reliable transport systems. This booklet also highlights the need for more inclusive systems to green transportation, with visible economies and social benefits.

## Consultative process is key to designing urban transport systems in a way that it empowers women and girls.

Gender-responsive planning is essential to ensure that women’s needs are met. It is important to engage women in decision-making processes related to transport planning and design. This includes ensuring that women are involved as customers and decision-makers, 25% of EV buyers in India are women. Women create almost half of the workforce, managing logistics, running small businesses, and working in the informal sector. Women’s needs and priorities vary widely across different contexts.
